#3014. Jon Horst has done enough damage

As the buzzer sounded against the Lakers last week to hand the Bucks their fourth straight drubbing, a range of feelings came over me: sadness, helplessness, anxiety, and, quite frankly, anger. It wasn't because the team was losing; I fully expected that. The first year of any rebuild always includes heaps of losing. No, it's *waves arms wildly* everything else that's going on—the bigger picture.

You see, what keeps supporters of a rebuilding team going is the future, but when you look at what the post-Giannis Bucks have—or, more importantly, what they don't have—there's certainly not heaps to get excited about. We know the draft is basically off the table until 2031, when Milwaukee regains its pick rights. The Bucks did get some potentially juicy Miami picks in the Giannis deal, which they could use to trade back into the upcoming drafts, but that remains theoretical; the only other avenue to get back in would be through spinning some or all of Tyler Herro, Jaime Jaquez Jr., and potentially even Ryan Rollins, but none of those guys will yield anything too flashy.

Among the players the Bucks have in-house as plausible future building blocks, Brayden Burries looks, at the very least, like a long-time starter, so that's comforting. Nate Ament is growing by the day—figuratively and maybe even literally—but it is still unclear whether he can transition from "intriguing talent" to "full-time winning player." I am getting more and more confident that Kasparas Jakucionis will become something, while Kel'el Ware, Ousmane Dieng, and Bogoljub Markovic remain much more of a question mark, and at this point, you'd probably bet against all three of them becoming long-term contributors. Finally, there's Ryan Rollins, who, at 24, may or may not be part of the future, but is someone I remain high on. That's… kinda it? Seven guys, most with a wide range of outcomes, and no blue-chip prospect as you enter the next four drafts without control over your first-round pick. Not ideal.

But it doesn't end there. Beyond the young core not being anywhere near as robust as you'd hope, Horst also made some cataclysmic free-agent signings that will further impede the rebuild. Exhibit A: Myles Turner, who signed to a four-year, $108m deal with Milwaukee last summer as a key part of Horst's last-ditch attempt to construct a winner around Giannis. Of course, to make this happen, the Bucks GM had to waive Damian Lillard and stretch the remaining $113m on his contract, putting $20m in dead cap space on Milwaukee's books for the next five seasons. Don't forget that Horst also had to send two second-round picks to Charlotte so they would absorb Pat Connaughton's contract, freeing up the requisite cap space to sign Turner.

LAS VEGAS, NV – JULY 11: Jon Horst and Doc Rivers of the Milwaukee Bucks Introduce Myles Turner during a Press Conference at the 2025 NBA Summer League game on July 11, 2025 at the Thomas & Mack Center in Las Vegas, Nevada. NOTE TO USER: User expressly acknowledges and agrees that, by downloading and or using this photograph, User is consenting to the terms and conditions of the Getty Images License Agreement. Mandatory Copyright Notice: Copyright 2025 NBAE (Photo by Ryan Stetz/NBAE via Getty Images) | NBAE via Getty Images

Suffice to say, getting Turner in the door was hard work! Surely Horst can trade him for something decent, given the extreme lengths he went to sign him? Nope. The 12-year veteran has been so diabolical that Milwaukee would struggle to even get neutral value for him at this point, with Brew Hoop founder Frank Madden recently calling Turner "easily the Bucks' worst ever free agent signing." Bravo. And hand up, after the decision was made that Giannis would not be traded, I was a fan of the Turner signing. I saw him play a key role on a team that had just gone to the NBA Finals and thought he would be Brook Lopez on steroids. Clearly, I was wrong. In my defence, I'm also not paid millions to have the purported ability to make these decisions.

And then there's the granddaddy of them all: circumventing the salary cap to sign Gary Trent Jr. No, you're not going delirious; you heard that right. Circumventing. The salary cap. To sign Gary Trent Jr. Predictably, just hours after Horst inked Trent to a definitely-not-shady $64m contract following a career-worst season, the NBA announced it would investigate the deal for possible cap circumvention and, according to Brian Windhorst, "everyone in the league expects a guilty finding." To add insult to injury, The Athletic reports that the penalty could be just as severe—if not more severe—than what the LA Clippers received.

Honestly, I'm not sure I'm ready to consider the degree to which any level of severe penalty could hamstring a small-market team like Milwaukee, which already traded most of its picks away. It would be franchise-altering for sure, and some would say franchise-dooming. All of this to secure the services of Gary… Trent… Junior. I keep repeating it because it almost doesn't sound real. Like, to be clear, GMs should always play by the rules, but to circumvent the cap for what? A ninth man on a good team? That's next-level foolish. At least the Clippers got their money's worth! Horst saw Trent averaging 14 and two in Toronto with non-existent defence and thought, "I simply must have this guy, and I'm willing to cheat to get his signature." What was he thinking?

So there. The Bucks are in a deep hole, and based on what we're seeing, it'll be a while before they can climb out. Of course, the main reason for that is that the org continually mortgaged the future for the present throughout the Giannis era, which, to be clear, I was largely on board with! I was in on mortgaging the future for Jrue Holiday. I was in on mortgaging the future once more for Damian Lillard (even if, in hindsight, way more consideration should have gone to whether Lillard was the right fit next to a superstar who lacked the exact intangibles needed to maximise him).

But you don't get carte blanche to operate that way forever. I know I've already bagged on about this, so I'll keep it brief, but Horst emptied the clip for Dame—that was (or should have been) his final bullet. Once the moment came when any reasonable person looked at Milwaukee and deduced that their title window had very likely closed, it was incumbent on the Bucks GM to capitalise on the one golden ticket he had left to salvage the future of this franchise after years of mortgaging it: trading Giannis from a position of strength. Unfortunately, that moment came when Lillard tore his Achilles less than two years after he arrived, much sooner than anyone had hoped.

MILWAUKEE, WISCONSIN – APRIL 27: Damian Lillard #0 of the Milwaukee Bucks is helped off the court after an apparent injury during the first quarter in Game Four of the Eastern Conference First Round NBA Playoffs against the Indiana Pacers at Fiserv Forum on April 27, 2025 in Milwaukee, Wisconsin. NOTE TO USER: User expressly acknowledges and agrees that, by downloading and or using this photograph, User is consenting to the terms and conditions of the Getty Images License Agreement. (Photo by Stacy Revere/Getty Images) | Getty Images

Still, the harsh reality wasn't going away, even if Horst wanted to ignore it and double down, which was exactly what he did. The product we're watching now—along with many of the restrictions that will hamstring the org's ability to improve it in the future—is largely a direct result of that decision, which forced Horst into negotiating from a position of weakness when he did pull the trigger on a Giannis trade.

And look, I understand and respect the opposing point of view: that it was always worth keeping your franchise GOAT for one final run while at least keeping the door ajar for Horst to pull another rabbit out of his hat that would convince the two-time MVP to re-sign. I get it. I would just say the NBA moves incredibly fast, and if you do not capitalise on opportunities when they are presented, you will get left behind—that's exactly what I fear has happened to Milwaukee by playing out the string with Giannis. In any event, and regardless of the decision to keep Giannis for that final season, enough evidence has piled up to show Horst lacks the poise and decision-making chops to do this job.

We've talked ad-nauseum about the nuances of Horst's career in the big chair. He's certainly had some major wins, and not all the losses people blame on him were actually his fault. Of course, he deserves immense credit for building the nucleus that ended a 50-year title drought in Milwaukee. Immense. But you can't dine out on that forever while ignoring the litany of blunders since then: the coaching hires, draft misses, contract misevaluations, and worst of all, the bewildering willingness to play Russian roulette in pursuit of signing wholly inconsequential players. Heck, the cap circumvention is a fireable offence on its own, let alone all the other stuff!

The truth is that Horst's decisions since the title weren't those of a level-headed operator; they were the moves of a riverboat gambler desperately chasing losses with bigger, bolder bets, the stakes rising with each slipup. Loss after loss after loss, culminating in what I am comfortable labelling one of the worst and most inexplicable gambles in NBA history. A decision, mind you, that fans will be forced to bear the brunt of as their team gets pushed even further down the already-deep hole Horst put it in.

Look around. The most successful teams share one thing: competent management. It's no coincidence that the Thunder, time after time, hit on the exact same picks the Bucks routinely miss on; it's no coincidence that the Celtics consistently take other teams' trash and turn it into their treasure. Equally, it's no coincidence that the Kings and Pelicans have spent decades as the laughingstock of the league. Competent management matters, and if the Bucks are serious about being taken seriously, the first step seems quite obvious.

#3010. Lions, Bucs injury designations: Cade Mays ruled OUT

The Detroit Lions (7-2) and the Tampa Bay Buccaneers (3-6) have declared their injury designations ahead of their Week 11 matchup at Ford Field.

The Lions held a walkthrough on Friday, their second of the week, and coach Dan Campbell noted that this lighter schedule was designed to allow the players to get some proper rest in their current schedule—after returning home from Germany, the Lions had to quickly adjust back to the Michigan time zone for this Sunday, and then play on short rest next week, as they play again on Thursday (Thanksgiving).

"This is a stretch we're in right now, and you've got to make sure that they're sharp enough; the sword is sharp enough to do what it needs to do, but we can't dull this blade over this three-week period," Campbell said on Friday. "And so yeah, we've kind of pulled back a little bit and the mental has gone way up and that's where the focus has got to be, making sure we're locked in on our responsibility, our job. It's important."

While there are no real surprises for those following this week's injury reports, Let's take a look at what we know.

Note: Any changes from the most recent injury report will be bolded.

Mays will miss his third game in a row, and with the Lions' next game on Thursday, they will likely only hold walk-through practices, so he won't have a chance to fully test his ankle before the Bears come to town. With that in mind, it wouldn't be surprising to see him miss the Thanksgiving game as well, and possibly target a return in Week 13 at Atlanta, if he is healthy enough.

With Mays sidelined, the Lions will continue to roll with Juice Scruggs at center. Scruggs has been terrific this season, though he'll have his hands full this week lining up across from Bucs' NT Vita Vea.

CB D.J. Reed (hip) — won't rush back, unlikely to play vs. Bucs

Vaki has played through his ankle injury the last two games and is expected to be available again this Sunday, barring any setbacks between this report and game time.

Lacy is expected to be a big part of the Lions game plan this week, given his prowess for stopping the run. Fortunately, the Lions plan to play Lacy's backup, Ahmed Hassanein, this week, so they'll have support if Lacy needs to take some reps off.

Bachie's status isn't totally clear right now, but limited practices all week are a good sign he could contribute on special teams.

Reed said earlier in the week that he won't rush back from this injury like he did last season because he doesn't want to be a "hindrance" to his teammates, who are playing at a high level right now. He plans to take his time, listen to trainers, and return when everything lines up. That could mean he plays this week, but Thanksgiving seems more likely.

Dorsey required attention from the training staff last Sunday, but he's been able to practice in limited fashion this week and looks like he'll have a chance to play against the Bucs.

Rakestraw has gotten in limited practices this week, so it appears he'll also have a chance to play on Sunday.

Joseph is set to play his first game since Week 5 of the 2025 season, and the All-Pro safety insists that he's ready, won't need to knock off any rust, and is still the same player he's always been. Campbell echoed that sentiment at his Friday press conference, noting that he's shown his previous range level in practices, and he's encouraged Kerby to just let the game come to him and not try to do too much.

"I know he's very eager, as you can imagine," Campbell explained. "It's been a long time. He's put in a ton of work to get back to this point; it's just play within the system. Don't try to do too much. Let the game come to you. Just play the safety position, play the call, trust the guys next to you. And don't try to go outside of that right now because I know it's been a long time. So that'll be it. And every rep that he gets, he'll settle in; he'll be just fine."

#3009. Federal Reserve Board announces pricing, effective January 1, 2027, for payment services the Federal Reserve Banks provide to banks and credit unions, and changes to streamline and adjust their check services

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 1:30 p.m. EST Share

The Federal Reserve Board on Thursday announced pricing, effective January 1, 2027, for payment services the Federal Reserve Banks provide to banks and credit unions, such as the clearing of checks, automated clearing house (ACH) transactions, instant payments, and wholesale payment and settlement services.

By law, the Federal Reserve must establish fees to recover the costs, including imputed costs, of providing payment services over the long run. The Federal Reserve expects to recover 101.5 percent of actual and imputed expenses in 2027, including the return on equity that would have been earned if a private-sector firm provided the services. Overall, price changes for 2027 will result in an estimated 1.4 percent average price increase for established, mature services.

In addition, the Federal Reserve Banks announced changes to streamline and adjust their check services. As described in the request for information on check services issued last year, the volume of checks processed by the Federal Reserve Banks is declining, but checks remain an important payment instrument for many consumers and businesses. The changes were informed by public feedback and ensure that the Federal Reserve Banks continue to provide check services efficiently. The adjustments include optimizing check processing windows during low-volume periods, increasing fees that banks and credit unions pay to process checks, and other operational updates. These changes do not impact consumers' and businesses' ability to pay with or receive checks and only impact how banks and credit unions process checks with Federal Reserve Banks.

The entire 2027 fee schedule for all of the Federal Reserve's payment services is published on FRBservices.org®. Additional information on the Federal Reserve's payments services, including service usage statistics, is available on the Board's website.

For media inquiries, please email [email protected] or call 202-452-2955.â¯â¯

#3007. NBA one-month awards! MVP, best rookie, breakout stars and surprise teams so far

With the NBA season hitting the one-month mark, let's hand out some early awards! Who has been the most valuable player so far? The best rookie? The league's breakout star? Our writers weigh in with their picks.

Kelly Iko: Victor Wembanyama, with a sliiiight edge over Luka Dončić. Of players that have registered at least 300 minutes so far, the Spurs are 42.5 points per 100 possessions better with him on the floor, the most impactful in the league by some distance. (San Antonio looked like a fish out of water earlier this week when he was on the bench vs. Detroit.) He's also realized that, at 7-foot-4, his rim presence should be consistently weaponized. Per Second Spectrum, Wemby logged 65 alley-oop dunk attempts all of last season; he's at 40 before Thanksgiving. Add this growth to his continued defensive wizardry, along with taking care of the ball and improving as a creator, and you have yourself a solid argument.

(Dillon Minshall/Yahoo Sports illustration)

Steve Jones: Luka Dončić. At this check-in, I have to go with Dončić after a month full of a variety pack of stepback 3s. That's 38 points a night with defenses attempting to throw multiple schemes at him and it just hasn't mattered a ton yet. Combine that with him "doing his job" on defense, with more activity on switches and rotations; he's a big reason why the Lakers are at the top of the West.

Dan Devine: Luka Dončić. I'm with Steve, give me the league's leading scorer, the reason the Lakers have been consistently vying for the No. 1 offense in the NBA, and the driving force behind an L.A. team that couldn't get out of the first round last year being neck-and-neck with the conference champion Spurs and East-leading 76ers for the league's best record. For more information, consult your local library:

Nekias Duncan: Victor Wembanyama. I don't think you can go wrong with Dončić or Wembanyama, honestly, but 28-12-3-1-4 on 62 true shooting is absolutely nuts. Wemby's dunking everything in sight, making teams pay when they send help — his assist rate has risen, while his turnover rate has dropped from last year — and he's pretty easily been the most destructive defender in basketball this year. The "off" portion of Wemby's on-off numbers are insane, but I'd redirect to the "on" portion — the Spurs are outscoring teams by over 28 points per 100 possessions with him on the floor.

Duncan: Cameron Boozer. With all due respect to Yaxel Lendeborg and Darius Acuff Jr. (don't let them boo you, big dawg), I'll also be rolling with Mr. Boozer. I've been enamored with his processing speed and versatility all year long. He attacks or passes ahead of tilted defenses at a high level already. He's also showcased a willingness to let it fly from deep, a necessary buffer while he gets comfortable with his inside-the-arc work (he's converting 60% of his 2s over his last six games) against bigger players.

Jones: Cameron Boozer. Rookie of the Year is going to continue to heat up as this season goes on, but Boozer's versatility offensively has delivered consistent impact for the Grizzlies. His ability to playmake fits right in with their identity: he can punish switches, and he can make plays on the short roll. There has rarely been a game where he hasn't left an imprint for a Grizzlies team that just keeps winning.

Devine: Yaxel Lendeborg. I'm firmly on the record as very on-board for Boozer, who became the second-youngest player in NBA history to record a triple-double, but I'll give a shout out to Lendeborg. Yes, it's been a pretty brutal week for him, and for the Warriors as a whole, since Stephen Curry went down. But what he showed in his first dozen games as a pro was truly eye-opening. Coming out of Michigan, the book on Yaxel was that, as a 24-year-old super-duper-senior, he'd have the experience, toolkit and physical maturity to be able to immediately fill a role in the league. I'm not sure anyone saw that role being "guy who can average an efficient 20-plus a night on high usage, and help elevate a team that just about everybody thought was drawing dead." That might not last forever, especially if Steph's absence stretches. But for the first month, it was an exceedingly pleasant surprise.

Iko: Darius Acuff Jr. I'm not going to punish Acuff for being born into the Kings family. I'm also not going to magnify his low shot-making efficiency or assist-to-usage ratio in Sacramento. This kid is an absolute terror with the ball in his hands, averaging 26.2 points, 7.0 assists and 3.6 rebounds a night. In spite of Sacramento. You wanna know the list of folks to achieve benchmarks like that as a rookie? Wilt. Kareem. Jordan. Oscar Robertson. Elvin Hayes. Darius. His feel for the game, creation and scoring versatility is there — the rough edges will smoothen out as time goes on.

Jones: Memphis Grizzlies. While the Sixers keep finding ways to win as they figure it out, and while the Lakers are at the top of the West, and the Wolves have stayed steady without Anthony Edwards, the Grizzlies being one game out of the top is something few saw coming. They are 8-0 on the road, which is not just the best road start in franchise history, but the longest road winning streak in franchise history. Let that sink in.

Duncan: Houston Rockets, if for no other reason than to save you all from the massive victory lap I'd like to take over the Grizzlies. It's not a surprise that the Rockets are a good team; the way they've been good is simply not what I had on the bingo card. They have the league's best offense and a top-five half-court unit to boot — a notable shift from last year (7th overall, 19th in the half-court). This largely hasn't been the Kevin Durant show; this is the second lowest scoring (21.3) and lowest shot-taking (13.3 attempts) season of his career. The growth and empowerment of Alperen Şengün and Amen Thompson, the confidence and shot-making of Jabari Smith Jr. (career-best 38.8% from 3), and the return of the steady-handed Fred VanVleet has led to a more efficient, free-flowing offense.

Iko: Houston Rockets. They have very quietly made a compelling case for being a top-5 team in the NBA. VanVleet's return has come with less control than he's accustomed to, but when you're converting shots at the highest efficiency of your career, who cares about usage rates? Smith is more confident in his in-between game and still has a knack for timely momentum-shifting splashes — as well as an unheralded five-position defensive skill set. They are second in offense and seventh in defense, don't turn the ball over, obliterate opponents on the offensive glass and still have a healthy Durant. Why couldn't they stick around until June?

Devine: Portland Trail Blazers, in part because I'm an incorrigible shill who wants to deliver a ruthless plug for the big story he just wrote on Damian Lillard, and in part because I'm not sure anybody saw Portland sitting at 10-5 with a top-10 defense heading into opening night. Micah Nori's done a sensational job, Donovan Clingan and Toumani Camara look like bona fide linchpins, Jrue Holiday is always just what the doctor ordered, and Scoot Henderson even broke out against the Bucks on Thursday. Get Ja Morant back on the court, and Portland might just have a stew goin'.

Devine: Toronto Raptors. It's not a shock to me that they are 5-10, given the myriad injuries they've suffered — most notably, obviously, getting just two games out of Kawhi Leonard before he went down with amorphous, ill-defined knee woes that, in a recent report by the great Michael Grange of Sportsnet, evoked the phrase "mystery condition." (Who could have foreseen this?!?) I do find it disappointing, though, that a team that won 46 games with the point differential of a 50-win squad and a top-seven defense last season — without Leonard — has so often looked so feckless this season … and that it's a combined 0-8 against teams with .500 or better records, getting blitzed by 11.5 points per 100 possessions against opponents with top-10 net ratings. Maybe, with better health, the Raptors will turn things around. Right now, though, even given the absence of their big-ticket offseason acquisition, they seem disappointingly unserious.

Iko: Charlotte Hornets. There's no way you could have convinced me the Hornets would be 28th in net rating a week before Thanksgiving. I know the injuries to Kon Knueppel and Coby White have delayed what should have been a smooth start. But there's just something off about them when you watch, a level of disharmony and disjointedness that shouldn't be there this early in a season. Their collapse this week on the road in L.A., a game in which they led quite comfortably by 18 points deep into the second half, was embarrassing. The erasure of Brandon Miller, who has suddenly turned into a hybrid of Monta Ellis and J.R. Smith, is also jarring. Charles Lee has quite the tall task on his hands.

Duncan: Utah Jazz, by default. Injuries have simply derailed this group. The playmaking and screening chops of Jusuf Nurkić were missing to begin the year, an underrated hit to what the Jazz want to establish. More pressing, they've had to navigate absences from Jaren Jackson Jr. and Lauri Markkanen. It's led to an offense that has felt rudderless at times — and easy to switch against — paired with a defense that isn't good enough to hold things together. I simply didn't think this would be a three-win team a month in.

Jones: Cleveland Cavaliers. Per NBA.com, they are the No. 4 scoring team in the league (118.3), boasting the No. 5 offensive rating in the league (117.5), and it doesn't feel like it at all. For a team with this level of firepower, with the added bonus of continuity after an Eastern Conference finals run, the drink has felt flatter than usual. The Cavs likely just need a quick chat with momentum to turn things around, but I had high expectations for this group to take off or figure things out quickly. It has felt stuck in the middle to me.

Iko: Paul George. If this is intended for younger players, I apologize, but I'm in complete awe of what 36-year-old is doing right now in Boston. He's played next to Jayson Tatum for only a month, but their synergy screams of a lifelong partnership. He's still firing off eight 3s a night while converting them at the highest clip of his career; still serving as an effective secondary creator, scorer and connective tissue for MazzullaBall; and still one of the premier contest-without-fouling wings around. This was supposed to be a puzzling Celtics transaction, remember? Not acquiring a player nearing 40 who's still producing at a top-25 level. Color me shocked.

Jones: Davion Mitchell. Just the term breakout star had me in a headlock; I wanted to go with KAT, but he's already broken out, Cooper Flagg has taken a leap, Franz Wagner is scoring and playmaking, and LaMelo Ball is playing defense. I'm going with Mitchell, leaning on the breakout portion of the program. He's been dynamic in transition and in pick-and-roll, finding his way into the paint consistently. The on-ball pressure remains tremendous and he's knocking down 3s. He's been exactly what the Heat have needed.

Duncan: Cedric Coward. (Davion Mitchell is such a tremendous shout!) Coward has simply been awesome for the Grizzlies to start the year. He's shooting the leather off the ball (45.2% on 5.2 attempts from 3), the drives have more juice, the passing has perked up, and he continues to add value as an offensive rebounder. On the other end, he's putting together a legitimate All-Defense campaign while consistently taking on tough assignments. As of this writing, opposing offenses are generating under 0.8 points per possession whenever Coward defends a ball screen (0.76), a handoff (0.76), or an off-ball screen (0.55). There's a reason the Grizzlies have been nearly 15 points better per 100 possessions with him on the floor.

Devine: Holy crap, none of you guys said Ty Jerome? OK, then: Ty freaking Jerome!

There are five players in the NBA this season averaging at least 25 points and five assists per game on .600 true shooting, and Ty Jerome is the only one of them that 99% of NBA fans couldn't pick out of a lineup. (The other four: Luka, Giannis, Tyrese Maxey, Jamal Murray.) Sickos who do this for a living might have been aware that the eighth-year pro out of Virginia could be capable of this sort of production given health and opportunity, but it's certainly come as a shock to just about everybody else — including opponents of the Grizzlies who might've come into the season expecting things to be sweet only to find themselves on the business end of a steady diet of impossible floaters and 30-foot pull-up bombs. Memphis has been one of the best stories in the league this season, and its low-profile, high-octane point guard has been one of the biggest reasons why.

#3004. Agencies extend comment period for proposed third-party risk management guidance

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 4:15 p.m. EST Share

The Federal Deposit Insurance Corporation, the Federal Reserve Board, the National Credit Union Administration, and the Office of the Comptroller of the Currency today announced that they will extend, until December 16, the comment period for proposed guidance on managing risks associated with third-party relationships. Comments on the proposal were originally due November 16.

The proposed guidance reflects the agencies' supervisory experience and lessons learned from examining financial institutions' third-party risk management practices. It is intended to help banks and credit unions tailor their risk management practices to the risks associated with individual third-party relationships. The proposed guidance takes a principles-based approach and, like all supervisory guidance, is non-binding.

When finalized, the federal bank regulatory agencies plan to rescind existing third-party risk management guidance and replace it with the finalized guidance to promote consistency and prudent innovation in the banking industry. NCUA also plans to rescind its existing third-party risk management guidance and will conduct its own reviews to update related guidance as needed.

Also today, the Federal Reserve Board separately announced that it will extend, until December 16, the comment period on a proposed third-party risk management guide specifically for Federal Reserve-supervised traditional community banks.

#3002. Knicks Bulletin: ‘I was raised by Wolves’

I thought yesterday's game was over by the end of the third quarter…

…only for these fools to nearly drop it. Good thing, they did not.

Here's a bunch of quotes coming off Minnesota as the boys return home once and for all.

On finishing the road trip with a victory over Minnesota:

"Great to finish off a road trip, getting a win in a pretty tough environment by a well-coached team. It'll make the plane ride a little bit better going home."

On the Knicks' fourth-quarter offensive collapse against the Timberwolves:

"Our execution the first three quarters was at a pretty high level against this team. And then you give them credit. Fourth quarter, they did a phenomenal job defensively, kind of bogged us down. The ball and the pace offensively in the frontcourt wasn't popping like it was the first three quarters."

On the Knicks winning the battle for loose balls despite struggling offensively in the fourth quarter:

"The thing that saved us was winning the 50-50 ballgame. We were 13-for-14 in that area, and that was huge for us because we had trouble scoring in the fourth."

On beating his former team as an NBA champion with the Knicks:

"I was raised by Wolves, but it was Knicks in 5 at the end."

On why the Knicks don't need to win games convincingly:

"For 'Dancing with the Stars.' For us, at the end of the day, as long as we win, this team is just trying to stack the number on the left side."

On the importance of winning games in different ways:

"If you wanna win a championship, you gotta be ready to win in any way possible, because you never know what the game will be."

On his uncertain contract situation and remaining focused on his responsibilities to the Knicks:

"I got a job to do, at the end of the day. I gotta come here and put that jersey on and do what's best for my brothers and the guys they have in my locker room. So, I'm just here to do my job."

On refusing to let his contract situation distract him from performing:

"I gotta put that jersey on and put the best performance out there for my teammates. So, that's all I really think about."

On his emotional attachment to Minnesota:

"I still have so much love for this place. It doesn't go away. I think it's actually grown as I've been away. I think that, even for me and my dad, we were just driving around the city and just going to places. We went to eat at a spot we love to go here. It always hits home. This is the last place that I got to play basketball with my mom. I got to see the kids be young before now. Jolani, my niece, who was in my rookie of the year photo actually in here, she was a baby, and now she's in high school. It's a different part of life, but it's a part of life that I really enjoy."

On returning to Minnesota as an NBA champion:

"It's always great to be home and to be back here. Nine years of my life, some amazing years, amazing moments. Yeah, it's special to be back. Obviously, it's bittersweet to come back with a championship because I wanted to win it here so bad for the city, for Minneapolis, for Minnesota, for all the people who haven't seen an NBA championship here. But it's great to be able to come back and to be able to see friends, people I call family here."

On the release of the new Grand Theft Auto game and his excitement to play it:

"I haven't played the game yet, but I've heard it's a hell of a game. So shout-out to Rockstar [Games] and 2K and Take-Two [Interactive]; they did a good job with that one. It's fun, man. The world is taken over by 'GTA,' as it rightfully should be."

On his Grand Theft Alvarado nickname and his history with the video game:

"I was a big fan of it before my name. It was just a cool name that I had, so it makes it even better when I play the new one. Got the name for it; now, play the game."

On how much his life has changed since GTA V dropped:

"Thirteen years ago, I wouldn't even be talking to you, I promise you that. It's a long journey, fun journey, one hell of a ride. Still with it."

On the Knicks nearly blowing their lead against Minnesota:

"It was hectic. We gotta get better at finishing a game like that. But it's a win, 3-1 on the road trip. We'll take it."

On the criticism Karl-Anthony Towns faced during his Timberwolves years:

"Particularly when he started his playoff journey, how much flak he had gotten."

On Towns leading the Wolves to the Western Conference Finals:

"We don't do it without him in all facets of the game, whether it be his defense in the Phoenix series, the only guy who held us in the game in Game 7 in Denver when we had nothing else going until we could find it in the second half. Those are the little moments that spring to mind. There are many others along the way. And he kind of grew into that and then was able to build on that with an experienced team where he fit into in New York."

On watching Towns succeed in the Knicks' championship run:

"It was fun to see him at the heart of it, too. Especially as they were kind of tweaking their offense going along and all of his skills came to the surface through that."

On the similarities between Towns and LaMelo Ball:

"They play with a lot of expression to their game and people interpret that or they don't like that. But it doesn't mean that A) they're not super talented players, of course, and B) they can't play winning basketball. Sometimes it's as much of the environment that people might be judging them unfairly through, if that makes sense.

"They both are really selfless in the way that they play. But maybe some of their expressions and the way that they carry themselves on the court draws a lot of attention and they get judged unfairly for that, too. They're actually incredibly selfless. They both play with a lot of joy.

"They're incredible people. Just great teammates, fun to be around, always bring the same energy every day. Very coachable."

#2996. Fantasy Basketball Weekend Streamers: Kevin Huerter to step up with Duncan Robinson sidelined

With Duncan Robinson set to miss at least a week with a quad injury, Kevin Huerter has slid into the starting unit as the floor spacer in his place. The first two starts for Huerter were fairly quiet, but he had a really strong four-game stretch before that. At least for their two games this weekend, with one of those coming against a Hawks team that is currently second in the league in pace, Huerter should continue to get the nod.

His role has expanded significantly over Detroit's last six games, and he has averaged 14.5 points, 3.3 rebounds, 3.8 assists, 1.0 steal, 1.7 blocks and 1.3 triples per game. He isn't a high-volume shooter, though it wouldn't be the worst thing if he became one, at least while Robinson is out. However, not many shooters can provide as much value in other categories as Huerter does.

The Hawks, Celtics, Bulls, Mavericks, Nuggets, Pistons, Pacers, Pelicans, Knicks, Thunder, Magic, Kings, Jazz and Wizards all play two games this weekend, so prioritize those teams when deciding what players to stream.

It might be getting colder outside, but the Pelicans have been having a Poole Party recently. He's returned to the rotation since Dejounte Murray requested a trade, and he has scored in double figures in five straight games. He scored a season-high 22 points in their last game and has at least five dimes in four straight games. Poole hasn't been as fantasy relevant in New Orleans, and his place in the rotation has been inconsistent at times. Right now, he's playing a lot and playing well.

This is a risky play since Flemings is listed as questionable with a lumbar strain after missing the Hawks' last two games. If that's too big of a risk for you, try Aaron Wiggins, who started Atlanta's last game with Flemings out. CJ McCollum is set to miss a month with a calf injury, which means more minutes for Flemings (and Wiggins). Flemings is definitely a long-term option that is still rostered in too few leagues.

Okoro has been starting for Chicago since Caleb Wilson fractured his hand, and the last few games have actually been solid in the box score. He has averaged 12.3 points, 4.8 rebounds, 3.0 assists, 1.5 steals and 2.3 triples per game over Chicago's last four games. Okoro has never been a strong fantasy option, which is why you need to strike while the iron's hot. This probably isn't going to last long.

Mikal Bridges' early-season struggles are well documented, which has opened the door for Shamet to play a larger role, and he has taken advantage. Shamet is a solid rotation piece, though he really only provides buckets from beyond the arc consistently. They also play two teams that rank in the top 10 in the NBA in pace, which should mean extra shots for Shamet.

Martin has started eight of the Mavs' last nine games, and while he didn't produce much at first, he has been excellent over the last three, where he has averaged 11.7 points, 6.0 rebounds, 3.7 assists, 1.7 steals and 2.3 triples per game while shooting 61.9 percent from the floor. Injuries have prevented Martin from making much of an impact in fantasy basketball in recent years, but he's finally healthy and playing well.

Oubre has been in the starting lineup for Indiana's last four games, which has led to him averaging 15.8 points, 4.8 rebounds, 1.8 steals and 1.3 triples per game during this stretch. He should be a solid option as long as he continues to start, which should continue until Tyrese Haliburton returns. It was a slow start to the season for him, but Oubre is starting to pick up the production.

Williams has been in and out of the starting lineup for OKC recently, and while he hasn't done much scoring, he has been able to provide some steals recently. He isn't a high-upside play, but he has gotten steady minutes and contributed decent defensive stats while remaining efficient. There's no reason to consider Williams as a long-term option.

Bamba has started three straight games for the Jazz, and he has been the player that the Magic hoped they were taking with the No. 6 overall pick back in 2018. During this stretch, he has averaged 15.7 points, 10.3 rebounds, 1.0 steal, 1.3 blocks and 2.3 triples per game. Until Jusuf Nurkić returns, Bamba should continue to start, or at least play significant minutes. There hasn't been much clarity surrounding Nurk's return as of late, so while Bamba should be a good play this weekend, he may be worth keeping around longer than that.

Prioritize Bamba, but Hayes was still solid enough as a reserve to be picked up. The Federal Reserve Board on Friday released results from a survey of senior financial officers at banks about their strategies and practices for managing reserve balances. The Senior Financial Officer Survey is used by the Board to obtain information about banks' reserve balance management strategies and practices, their expectations around the size of their balance sheets and deposit balances their activity in money markets, and their views on other matters, such as operations on weekends and holidays.

The survey was conducted in collaboration with the Federal Reserve Bank of New York between September 18, 2026, and September 28, 2026, and includes responses from banks that held around three-fourths of total banking system reserve balances at the time of the survey.

#2993. NBA hot seat rankings for 6 head coaches who need to pick it up quick

NBA head coaches often seem like they are hired to get fired. Only seven head coaches league-wide started their current jobs before 2023, and just Erik Spoelstra (2008) and Steve Kerr (2014) were hired before the dawn of the 2020s. The coaching carousel never pauses for long, and that will likely be no exception this year.

Teams can no longer hide their failures behind the thin veil of tanking after new lottery reform rules went into effect for this season. When everyone is trying to win, some teams are destined to become disappointed, and that usually means a head coach could quickly be out of a job.

Six teams made head coaching changes for the 2026–27 season, and none of them are in danger of getting fired after just one month of play. Some of the others won't be so lucky. Here's an early look at the NBA hot seat landscape, and what these coaches need to do to buy themselves a little more time.

I'm a long-time believer in Fernandez's coaching ability. He made Cam Johnson look so good that the Nets got a first-round pick back for him from Denver, then he immediately helped Michael Porter Jr. enjoy the most productive year of his career. It's hard to hold Brooklyn's 46-118 record the last two years against the head coach when the organization was actively trying to tank. Unfortunately, the Nets didn't get any lottery luck, and now they are miserable again this season (3-11) in a year when they owe their first-round pick to the Houston Rockets. Fernandez signed a contract extension back in April, but it's at least a little unnerving that the Nets seem ready to fire his boss, Sean Marks, after 11 years at general manager. If and when Marks gets canned, a new GM will probably want to hire their own head coach, and that puts a lot of pressure on Fernandez to turn things around for the Nets this season — which is starting to feel like an impossible task.

The Jazz's grand tanking plan was finally supposed to be over when they landed the No. 2 overall pick and Darryn Peterson in the 2026 NBA Draft. So much for that: Utah has been decimated by injuries to their three best front court players — Lauri Markkanen, Jaren Jackson Jr., and Jusuf Nurkic — while stumbling their way to a brutal 3-13 start. Like Fernandez, Hardy's previous losing seasons were excused by the organization's long-term tanking plans, and it's possible the injuries will be enough to excuse this one. Hardy signed an extension through the 2030–31 season just over a year ago. I thought the Jazz had enough to sneak into the Western Conference playoffs this season, partially because I believed in Hardy's ability to coach up his group. If Utah stays one of the worst teams in basketball all year, though, there will probably be some degree of heat on his seat.

Credit the Nuggets for hanging tough so far this season when it feels like they're no longer at their best. Denver is only No. 15 in the league in net-rating, but they've out-performed that mark with a 8-5 record, including pulling out a tight win over the mighty Boston Celtics recently. I just wonder what a few bad weeks could do to David Adelman's job stability. There are only so many levers for Denver to pull with an older, expensive roster at this point, and the easier response to a downturn might be firing the head coach. Adelman has had the respect of a veteran team since being promoted from assistant ahead of the 2025 playoffs, but the Nuggets' pathetic showing in the playoffs last year — a six-game first-round loss to the Wolves — probably didn't do him any favors. I don't think Adelman is the problem in Denver, but the team will need to keep winning to avoid the temptation of trying out a new coach.

It feels like the Cavs are trending in a poor direction for Atkinson's job security. Cleveland went from 64 wins to 52 wins (and an extremely unconvincing conference finals berth) last season while making a win-now trade for James Harden. The Cavs are just .500 to start this year, and they still haven't answered most of the big questions that have dogged this team for years. Evan Mobley has never ascended to superstardom like I thought he might, and we still don't know if he can play the five full-time. Donovan Mitchell and James Harden will always be a liability defensively, and Harden's offense is finally slipping a bit at age-37. I love what the younger bench pieces have given the Cavs this year — Tyrese Proctor and rookie Meleek Thomas have been great — and it might be enough to save Atkinson for now. Still, with an owner who spends a ton of money above him, the Cavs could be looking for a new coach if they don't take off on a winning streak soon.

The Kings' decision to fire Mike Brown was asinine in the moment, and only looks worse a couple years later after Brown led the Knicks to an NBA championship. It's not all Doug Christie's fault that Sacramento has so quickly reverted to its ordinary form as the worst team in the league after Brown led the franchise to its only playoff appearance in 20 years, but he hasn't done much to help the situation either. The Kings are atrocious again, and it doesn't feel like a stretch to think they could challenge for one of the worst records in league history after a 1-13 start. Everything about this franchise needs to be burned save for Darius Acuff and some savvy moves on the margins of the roster, and ultimately Christie has no chance to survive. Hopefully the next Kings coach will be dealt a better hand, because I'm not sure anyone could have saved this roster.

The Wizards don't have any excuses this year. They landed the No. 1 overall pick and A.J. Dybantsa. They traded for veterans with All-NBA pedigrees in Anthony Davis and Trae Young. It feels like they have a thousand first-round picks on this roster with multiple years of experience. Brian Keefe has to win with this team after combining for only 35 victories over the last two years, and it's not happening yet. While the disastrous Trae Young contract isn't Keefe's fault, he also hasn't been able to unlock him yet. Former top-10 picks like Alex Sarr and Tre Johnson aren't showing much in the way of real progress. Unlike Fernandez and Hardy who have shown real flashes of being good coaches, Keefe just has nothing to really hang his hat on as he comes out of the tanking era with more losses. I don't think he's surviving the season unless the Wizards turn this thing around quickly, and I wouldn't be surprised if he doesn't even make it to Christmas.

#2992. Federal Reserve announces College Fed Challenge winners

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 4:15 p.m. EST Share

The University of California, Los Angeles won the 23rd Annual National College Fed Challenge.

The University of California, Los Angeles (UCLA) won the 23rd annual national College Fed Challenge on Friday, a team competition that encourages undergraduate students to learn about the U.S. economy, monetary policymaking, and the role of the Federal Reserve System. Teams analyze economic and financial conditions and formulate a monetary policy recommendation, modeling the Federal Open Market Committee. The team, from Los Angeles, California, represented the San Francisco District and included Aarushi Maheshwari, Annika Chan, Atul Prasanth, Caitlyn Cox, Finn Karish, Eli Brooks (alternate), Kennedy Yang (alternate), Manasi Vartak (alternate), Ronojoy Borpujari (alternate), and Sahil Sudani (alternate). The team's advisers were Chris Surro and Ali Haider Ismail.

"I am very happy to congratulate the students who competed in this year's College Fed Challenge, especially the winners and finalists," said Federal Reserve Chairman Kevin Warsh. "They took on the same big questions we policymakers face. I'm impressed by their talent, their rigor, and their eagerness to think hard about monetary policy and the economy."

This year's College Fed Challenge included 141 schools from 34 states across the nation, the most in the competition's history. Teams submitted video presentations or participated in local competitions last month. Twenty-one semi-finalist teams then participated in question-and-answer sessions held earlier this month, and seven finalists were invited to the Federal Reserve Board for the national finals. The other national finalists were the University of Chicago in second place and the University of Maryland, College Park in third place. Teams with honorable mentions include Cleveland State University, Harvard College, Pennsylvania State University, and St. Lawrence University.

Teams were evaluated on their economic analysis, responses to the judges' questions, teamwork, and presentations.

For media inquiries, please email [email protected] or call 202-452-2955.

Board of Governors of the Federal Reserve System

20th Street and Constitution Avenue N.W., Washington, DC 20551

#2976. Now what? Colts should have traded Daniel Jones when they had the chance

Here's the Daniel Jones timeline for the past eight months — and stick with us, because it's a wild ride.

The Colts signed Jones to the largest two-year contract in NFL history despite coming off Achilles surgery a few months earlier.

The Colts kept Jones as their starter, without any new competition, this season.

The Colts continued to put Jones as QB1 while he struggled, throwing interceptions in 10 of his 11 starts.

The Colts reportedly listened to trade offers for their starting QB prior to the Nov. 10 trade deadline before ultimately passing.

The Colts started Jones in Thursday's blowout loss to the Texans.

Whew. Caught up? Good. Now consider: Cutting Jones will cost the team nearly $15 million in dead salary-cap money, plus another $35 million for next year.

Friday's move was nothing short of a stunner, if only from a financial standpoint. From a football standpoint, the Colts couldn't justify keeping him as the starter? Cutting him now, knowing what a hit they'd take? It almost feels personal at this point.

Colts head coach Shane Steichen clearly had enough of him Thursday night. The speculation on why Steichen hadn't gone to Anthony Richardson Sr. sooner was that the coach might have trusted Richardson even less.

Steichen might also be in self-preservation mode, trying to hold together a ship that was partially stripped before the trade deadline and already in rough shape before that. But assuming Steichen didn't reach his breaking point during Thursday's game, why did the Colts wait to get rid of him?

And not only for nothing. It actually cost the Colts money and flexibility to release him now. It doesn't get much uglier than that.

Daniel Jones' run with the Colts ends after 24 starts in which Indianapolis went 11-13.

There have been credible reports that multiple teams inquired about Jones' availability before the trade deadline, including the Ravens and Vikings. Others might also have checked in on him. The Colts held firm. The Ravens got Jacoby Brissett and the Vikings landed Kirk Cousins.

This feels like a major missed opportunity. By releasing Jones, the Colts got nothing and made their situation worse. Even with the potential return of a Day 3 draft pick and a slightly better impact on their salary cap, the Colts passed.

It should be noted that the Vikings feel great about Cousins, and it's possible that their interest in Jones was more of a fallback option. The Ravens? Brissett's numbers over the past two seasons have been slightly better, so it's fair to say that they might have preferred the QB they ended up with.

But any potential interest in Jones should have been met with a take-him-off-our-hands type of offer — the kind a team couldn't turn down. Someone is going to sign him here in the coming days, and given all the QB injuries around the league, it wouldn't be a stunner to see Jones start a game at some point this season.

The Colts were more than willing to part with other assets prior to the deadline, including DeForest Buckner, Quenton Nelson and Josh Downs. They listened to offers for Jones and Jonathan Taylor but ended up keeping both. Taylor had 11 carries for 21 yards and four catches for six yards on Thursday. Jones struggled and was cut. Little of this makes sense.

When general manager Chris Ballard was fired midseason and replaced by interim GM Ed Dodds, it felt like an opportunity for Dodds to earn the full-time job by starting the process of replenishing the Colts' draft pool. They've done that, but they've also thrown away large chunks of meat still on the bone.

Dodds was dealt a tough hand (one he helped put together, for what it's worth), but it's arguable he has played it poorly — unless Jones' release was mandated from on high. That's possible. If it's true, the Colts' situation is even uglier than it appears.

Since Andrew Luck's shocking retirement in August 2019, the Colts have started 12 different quarterbacks. Jones' 24 starts were the most from that group in that span, but it's highly likely some new names are added to the list next season. This franchise is starting to become synonymous with QB instability, and after two decades of having Peyton Manning and Luck, that's a bitter pill to swallow.

The remaining options on the roster this season are Richardson and Riley Leonard. The Colts don't appear to have great belief in either one. They declined the fifth-year option on Richardson's rookie deal, making him a free agent this spring, and have been reluctant to give him much of a shot prior to now. Leonard was last seen in last year's Week 18 start and hasn't played a snap in 2026.

The assumption is that Richardson will play elsewhere next season. He certainly has a chance to rewrite his story in Indy as Jones' replacement, but whatever he does down the stretch will likely serve as an audition for his next stop. Leonard could be back in 2027, but it's unlikely he'd be anything higher than a backup to start next season.

The Colts could have waited until the offseason and made Jones a post-June 1 cut, with far less of a salary-cap footprint. The question of whether to keep Jones is settled, but the problems are only starting.

The Sauce Gardner trade cost them next year's first-round draft pick, and though the pre-deadline trades netted them some Day 2 and 3 ammunition over the next two years, it still makes acquiring a quarterback tougher come springtime.

The Colts can still move into the bottom of Round 1 for a quarterback in next spring's draft. But will there be a viable starting candidate available in that range? What was thought to be a banner QB crop in the 2027 NFL Draft is turning into more of a grab-bag situation, depending on which underclassmen declare.

Perhaps an unexpected veteran or two could hit the market. The veteran route feels more likely now, at least from an immediate-starter perspective. But will there be starting-quality options? That hope feels dubious now. Kyler Murray, Geno Smith and Deshaun Watson are the caliber of names that could be options. Do any of them elevate this team or give the Colts much more than a bridge option?

You can forget about trading for Lamar Jackson. The Colts don't have the assets to compete. That holds true for any other potential starting QB who could be available via trade.

The pathways to salvation appear quite limited. The head coach appears to be on thin ice. The Colts sunk major money into Taylor and Alec Pierce this offseason but have little to show for it.

In short, the whole situation is a hot mess.

#2961. Patriots' A.J. Brown reportedly expected to be out multiple weeks with hamstring injury

New England Patriots receiver A.J. Brown is expected to be sidelined for multiple weeks due to a hamstring injury, according to ESPN's Mike Reiss and Adam Schefter.

Brown, 29, injured his hamstring during Week 10's 35-10 loss to the Detroit Lions in Munich. He got hurt in the fourth quarter and did not return, finishing with two catches (on six targets) for 27 yards.

In his first season with the Patriots, Brown has already missed six of the team's 10 games. First, he sustained a high ankle sprain in the season opener and sat out four games while on injured reserve. Then he was sidelined for an additional two games due to a strained knee.

The news on Brown comes as the Patriots also officially announced that quarterback Drake Maye suffered a fractured right fibula against the Lions and could miss the remainder of the regular season.

New England dropped to 5-5 following the defeat versus Detroit and has lost three of its past four games. The Patriots have a bye for Week 11, then visit the Los Angeles Chargers (1-8) in Week 12. New England is still in the AFC playoff race, just one game behind the Cleveland Browns (6-4) for the conference's third wild-card berth.

With Brown injured, the Patriots' receiver depth chart consists of Mack Hollins, DeMario Douglas, Efton Chism III and Kyle Williams. Romeo Doubs could join them after the bye week when he's eligible to be activated from IR. Douglas is the team's leading pass-catcher among wideouts with 32 receptions for 438 yards and four touchdowns.

Brown was acquired from the Philadelphia Eagles during the offseason, trading a 2028 first-round pick and 2027 fifth-round selection. He registered 1,003 receiving yards last season with the Eagles (his lowest total in four seasons). However, Brown had reportedly become frustrated with quarterback Jalen Hurts — and his role in the Eagles' offense.

Philadelphia traded Brown before a three-year, $96 million extension kicked in that takes him through the 2029 season.

Brown is in his eighth NFL season, beginning his career with the Tennessee Titans. He was the team's second-round selection (No. 51 overall) out of Ole Miss and played with the team for three seasons.

The 6-foot-1 receiver was dealt to the Eagles during the 2022 NFL Draft for first- and fourth-round picks. He subsequently received a four-year, $100 million extension from Philadelphia.

A three-time Pro Bowler and two-time second-team All-Pro selection, Brown has 535 career receptions for 8,157 yards and 56 touchdowns.

#2958. Pistons vs Raptors preview: 2nd NBA Cup game

Early season Fridays = NBA Cup day! The Detroit Pistons can climb to 2-0 in group play with a win over the worse-than-expected Toronto Raptors (1-1 in group play). The NBA thought this would've been a big game, handing out the Prime Video first game slot, but injuries, injuries, injuries.

Ron Holland, Duncan Robinson, and Jalen Duren are out again, but Cade Cunningham is expected to play. Injuries have slowed down the Raptors a ton.

RJ Barrett and Collin Murray-Boyles won't suit up in Detroit. Kawhi Leonard remains out. His absence is the main source of Toronto's struggle, but I still expected more.

The Raptors are still live even though they should be better. That said, Detroit isn't a team that takes opponents lightly because of who's in or out. The Pistons play the same brand of ball regardless of who's out there on either side.

Wednesday's 122-109 win over the New Orleans Pelicans tripled down on that point. Even a shorthanded Pistons squad without Cade manages to find a way.

Daniss Jenkins. Javonte Green. Ausar Thompson with the ball. Paul Reed with extended minutes — trustworthy things.

We got Isaiah Joe back in the lineup in that one! One of the marquee signings of Detroit's offseason let it fly. Joe went 3-for-5 from range and put it on the floor, aiding Detroit's flow. Bball Paul said Joe had more in his game during media day. Paul knows ball.

The ability to leverage your shooting with live dribble decisions is an invaluable skill come playoff time. Love seeing Joe back out there, and more of that would be cool.

John Collins was on a heater (4-6 on 3s). He should get six 3s up every night with the room he has to fire. Collins will be matched up with Jakob Poeltl tonight. Poeltl is arguably the biggest hacker in the league. His 5.6 foul percentage ranks in the fourth percentile among bigs.

A lot of those Cade drives should end with trips to the charity stripe. Cade's .37 FTr is a career high. He's getting the calls and shooting ~86 percent at the line. That works. Cade is also putting together a strong All-Defense case, being a big-time contributor on the sixth-best defense.

Per Stathead, only Cade, Nikola Jokić, Matisse Thybulle, Scottie Barnes, and Caleb Wilson are averaging at least 0.9 blocks and 1.4 steals. Cade is doing this without fouling. He's leveling up even more on that end.

Barnes recently declared himself the best defender in the world. I have no problem with that. He is up there. He's a legit 1-5 defender. Anybody that good at something should feel they're the best. That confidence got them where they are.

Barnes is Barnes, but I thought Toronto would have a more intimidating team defense. Cleaning The Glass ranks them 17th overall and 19th over the past two weeks + they are on a six-game losing streak.

The injury bug cannot be ignored, but I thought Toronto would be immune to long stretches of defensive lapses because of their suffocating gap principles.

Those principles still force turnovers at a frightening rate (2nd), but they haven't dug in and clamped down like last year's Raptors team. They're still feisty enough to muddy up a game, but I expected more.

Toronto's off-the-bounce creation takes a big blow with no Kawhi and Barrett. Barnes has more shot-making responsibilities this season. His 23.8 points per game are a career high. Immanuel Quickley will get them up. Catching fire vs. Ausar and the Pistons' defense isn't easy, but Toronto is looking to Quickley to provide immediate offense.

Jamison Battle is one of Toronto's consistent snipers. He's not a shooter you can disregard and is coming off a 19-point, 5-for-10 from 3 game. Ja'Kobe Walter can shoot it too and is quietly turning into a dependable heavy-minute wing.

With a win, Detroit could be in first or second in the East Group A division. The Orlando Magic (2-0) take on the struggling Brooklyn Nets (0-1) tonight. Should Orlando snag a win, they stay atop the group.

One game and one week at a time for Detroit in this tournament. They smacked around the Nets in their first cup game and look to do the same against the Raptors.

Detroit Pistons (10-4): Cade Cunningham, Daniss Jenkins, Kevin Huerter, Ausar Thompson, John Collins

Toronto Raptors (5-10): Immanuel Quickley, Ja'Kobe Walter, Jamison Battle, Scottie Barnes, Jakob Poeltl

The New York Knicks won the NBA Cup tourney and the real thing last year. Did that add any validity to the tournament?

#2952. Kushner says Russia 'comfortable' with idea of NATO-like security guarantees for Ukraine

U.S. envoy Jared Kushner has said Russia may accept NATO-like security guarantees for Ukraine, adding that President Donald Trump wants to bring an end to Russia's full-scale invasion "with Europe by his side."

His comments come shortly after Trump, Russian President Vladimir Putin and Chinese President Xi Jinping held historic trilateral talks on the sidelines of the Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation forum, a meeting that did not appear to result in any concessions on Ukraine or European security.

"I think we've got the Russians comfortable with that concept where any security guarantee for Ukraine would be based on Article 5," Kushner said alongside European leaders at a news conference in Germany on Thursday, referring to the part of NATO's treaty that enshrines the principle of collective defense.

"I think that that comes with at least what the Russians are saying, which is their intention is, when this war is over, to not have another war in the future," he added. Kushner, who is Trump's son-in-law, was at the conference in Berlin alongside fellow U.S. envoy Steve Witkoff.

They have led the Trump administration's efforts to end Russia's nearly five-year-long war with Ukraine.

Article 5 defines an attack against one NATO member as an attack against all members. The alliance calls it a "unique and enduring principle that binds its members together, committing them to protect each other and setting a spirit of solidarity."

Ukraine has long held ambitions to join the alliance. Russia has previously said that if Kyiv joined NATO, it would cause problems for many years.

It was not immediately clear what form potential security guarantees would take, or whether Russia or Ukraine would accept them. Russia's Foreign Ministry, Ukraine's Foreign Ministry and NATO were not immediately available to comment when contacted by CNBC on Friday morning.

"From America's perspective, giving an Article 5-like security guarantee is a really, really big deal and I think that that is something that President Trump is open to, but obviously it has to be in America's interest to do that," Kushner said.

Russia launched a wave of missile and drone attacks on Ukraine's energy and transport infrastructure overnight, damaging three bridges in Kyiv and killing at least three people, authorities said.

Kyiv mayor Vitali Klitschko said via Telegram on Friday that Kyiv's Podilskyi, Darnytskyi and Southern crossings had been damaged by missile strikes, with experts currently assessing the damage.

Russia's Defense Ministry said it had carried out a "massive" strike on several Ukrainian cities, targeting enterprises of the country's defense industry and warehouses used in the production and storage of missiles and drones.

Putin vowed on Thursday that Russia would respond to Ukraine's attacks on the Kerch Bridge, adding it would be "much more painful." The bridge, which links Russia to occupied Crimea and is one of Putin's signature projects, was damaged by repeated Ukrainian attacks this week.

Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelenskyy on Tuesday described it as an "unprecedented operation" several weeks in the making, with Ukraine's defense forces, security services and weapons manufacturers involved.

#2950. F1 LIVE | Follow FP3 of the Las Vegas Grand Prix here!

Welcome to GPblog's live coverage of the third free practice session for the 2026 Formula 1 Las Vegas GP. After a remarkable opening session in which not a single driver managed to set a lap time, George Russell was able to top the times in FP2 under the lights of the Las Vegas Strip Circuit. Follow all the action from FP3 right here.

George Russell topped the second free practice session for the 2026 Las Vegas Grand Prix, setting a 1:47.272 to beat Pierre Gasly by just 0.071 seconds. Charles Leclerc completed the top three after dominating much of the session, while Max Verstappen eventually returned to the track following a lengthy spell in the garage.

Although the rain from FP1 stopped before the second session began, the track remained wet, forcing drivers to initially head out on full wet tyres.

It meant there was slippery conditions for the duration of the session, different conditions to what is expected in Las Vegas today.

Although Russell was quickest he pointed out that the McLarens and the Ferrari of Charles Leclerc showed perhaps the strongest pace. Leclerc was over three tenths down on Russell in third, while the McLarens of Oscar Piastri and Lando Norris finished P5 and P7 respectively.

Isack Hadjar was P4 for Red Bull, Lewis Hamilton P6, while Verstappen ultimately finished P13.

FP3 comes on the back of the news that there has been an unanimous agreement for V8s to return from the 2030 season.

V8s were last used in F1 in 2013 before the introduction of the 1.6-litre V6 hybrid power units, which started a new era for the sport.

In a FIA statement, president Mohammed Ben Sulayem described the return to V8s as a way to 'reconnect with the DNA of the championship'.

He said: "This agreement marks a significant milestone in shaping the future of Formula 1. From the outset I have been clear in my ambition to deliver a sport that is more competitive and more exciting for our fans, while remaining at the forefront of innovation and sustainability.

"The return of V8 engines represents an opportunity to reconnect with the DNA of this Championship, bringing back the sound, emotion and pure racing that is loved worldwide. Our vision is clear, lighter, simpler and more affordable cars that put driver skill at the heart of the competition."

#2949. Some FHA borrowers pay mortgage insurance for a decade or more. There's an industry push to change the rules

Homeowners with conventional loans who pay mortgage insurance typically can shed that expense — meant to protect a lender against loss if the borrower defaults — once they've built sufficient equity in their home. But that's not how it works for those with a Federal Housing Administration loan.

A fresh push to change the rules governing mortgage insurance for FHA loans came this week, after the Department of Housing and Urban Development released a report to Congress on Monday showing that the FHA's mortgage insurance fund remains financially strong. Its capital ratio — a measure of its financial cushion against potential losses — was 10.29% as of Sept. 30, down from 11.47% a year earlier, but still more than five times the 2% minimum required by Congress.

"The program's financial strength provides an opportunity for targeted changes that improve affordability for borrowers while preserving the long-term stability" of FHA's insurance fund, said Bob Broeksmit, president and CEO of the Mortgage Bankers Association, in a statement released after the report was issued. The trade group was one of a handful of organizations that put out statements calling for changes.

The push, which revives a longstanding mortgage industry request, also comes several weeks after President Donald Trump issued an executive order that included calling for the launch of a federal pilot program that would cancel mortgage insurance on certain conventional loans once borrowers meet equity requirements.

The order would apply generally to future loans backed by Fannie Mae and Freddie Mac — two government-sponsored enterprises that purchase qualifying mortgages from lenders and resell them as bundled securities. It wouldn't apply to FHA loans.

FHA mortgages are issued by private lenders but insured by the federal government, which protects lenders against losses if a borrower defaults. The loans are designed to make homeownership more accessible, particularly for borrowers with limited savings for a down payment or with lower credit scores.

Affordability challenges persist in the housing market, with elevated home prices and mortgage rates putting homeownership out of reach for many prospective buyers. Existing homeowners are also facing rising costs alongside their mortgages, including property taxes and homeowners insurance amid overall high inflation.

About 83% of new FHA purchase loans went to first-time homebuyers in the most recent fiscal year, according to the FHA report to Congress. Altogether, the FHA insured more than 8.4 million mortgages as of Sept. 30.

The median age of first-time buyers is 40, according to a new report from the National Association of Realtors.

FHA loans also require borrowers to pay mortgage insurance regardless of the size of their down payment. Under current rules established in 2013, FHA borrowers with a down payment that's less than 10% of the loan must pay mortgage insurance premiums for the duration of the loan. Those who put 10% or more down pay that insurance for 11 years.

Borrowers generally pay both an upfront mortgage insurance premium and an annual premium, which is typically divided into monthly payments. The upfront premium is usually 1.75% of the base loan amount and can be financed into the mortgage. For many borrowers with 30-year FHA loans, the annual premium is 0.55% of the outstanding loan balance.

Conventional mortgages generally don't require mortgage insurance unless the loan is for more than 80% of the home's value. In 2022, the premium typically ranged from 0.58% to 1.86% annually, according to Fannie Mae.

If those borrowers have to pay mortgage insurance, they can typically request its removal once they've built 20% equity in the home, or it is automatically canceled when their loan drops to 78% of the original value.

The mortgage industry has floated two different proposals.

One would reduce the rate that applies to FHA mortgage insurance. For example, the Mortgage Bankers Association recently suggested a rate reduction of 20 basis points, which would lower the standard 0.55% rate to 0.35% for new borrowers.

For example: On a $350,000 mortgage, a 0.55% rate generally translates into $1,925 annually, or about $160 monthly, at the start of the loan. As the principal owed slowly goes down over time through a borrower's monthly payments, so does that mortgage insurance amount.

At 0.35%, the annual premium would be $1,225 at the start of the loan, or $102 monthly — a difference of $58 per month.

The FHA last cut that rate in 2023, when it was reduced from 0.85%. At the time, HUD estimated that affected borrowers would save an average of $800 annually. However, in the FHA's new report to Congress, the agency noted that the 2023 cut has reduced the fund's revenue, contributing to a decline in its capital ratio.

The other industry proposal would implement auto-cancellation of premiums once the loan balance fell to 78% of the home's original value at purchase — in other words, the same as for Fannie- and Freddie-backed mortgages.

"Cancellation prevents the squeeze for borrowers who have paid down their loans and shouldn't be paying for coverage they have outgrown," said Michael Farrell, president of the National Association of Mortgage Brokers, a trade group that generally has supported the cancellation proposal.

"NAMB believes that cancellation at 78% protects the [FHA insurance fund], because premiums can restart if the [capital] ratio ever falls to 2%," Farrell said.

FHA mortgages received that treatment from 2001 through 2013, but the agency eliminated it to shore up its insurance fund after financial reserves deteriorated over time following the housing market crash, which led to the 2008 financial crisis.

Whether the proposals will go anywhere this time is uncertain. HUD, which oversees the FHA, did not respond to a CNBC email seeking more information.
