#2948. After Daniel Jones' Colts release, four NFL landing spots for veteran QB

Daniel Jones, it turns out, won't be the one to break the Indianapolis Colts' post-Andrew Luck retirement quarterback woes.

Jones was released by the team on Friday, Nov. 20, 24 games into a two-year stint with the Colts that started with an 8-2 record in 2025 before injury and inconsistency led to a spectacular derailment of the franchise's hopes of contending in the wide-open AFC South.

The Colts moved on from Jones, in favor of uber-athletic but raw backup Anthony Richardson Sr., one day after falling to 3-8 on the season with a 26-3 loss to the Houston Texans. The Colts offense continued to sputter with Jones at the helm against the Texans. He passed for 144 yards on 12-for-27 passing and threw his league-leading 13th interception.

Jones couldn't rediscover the promise that he showed in 2025 before a suffering a torn right Achilles tendon in Week 14. The eighth-year quarterback was lauded by coaches and teammates in recovering quickly enough to be the Colts' Week 1 starter in 2026, but his 58.4 completion percentage this season is the worst of his career, and his 7-to-13 ratio of touchdowns to interceptions tanked his quarterback rating to a career-low 30.3 before his release.

Still, Jones fits the mold of a competitive NFL quarterback at 6-foot-5 and 230 pounds, and despite this year's struggles with accuracy, he has thrown 96 career touchdowns to 68 interceptions. At 29, Jones has plenty of potential years left to fill a meaningful role with an NFL team.

Jones' long-term outlook under center isn't likely to shake out until the offseason, but here are four options where he could fit for the remainder of the season.

With franchise quarterback Drake Maye potentially lost for the season to a reported fracture to his right tibia, the Patriots are suddenly 5-5 and staring down an eight-game stretch with Tommy DeVito under center. You tell us that head coach Mike Vrabel isn't looking around the league for options. Jones is only slightly older than DeVito but has 93 career starts to DeVito's eight, and would be a viable option to quickly pick up the Patriots' playbook if DeVito doesn't grasp the opportunity.

We'll take the layup here, as the Vikings were said to be interested in Jones before trading with the Las Vegas Raiders for current starter Kirk Cousins ahead of the NFL trade deadline. With Cousins in place, Jones would simply be an insurance piece to break in case of injury. But at 7-2 and locked in the toughest divisional playoff race in the NFL, the Vikings would be wise to hedge against a future with either injured Kyler Murray or ineffective Carson Wentz steering the ship.

With the Ravens at 4-6, this season may be all over but the shouting. Injuries to star Lamar Jackson and backup Tyler Huntley forced the Ravens to trade for journeyman QB Jacoby Brissett ahead of the deadline, only to see him flop in a 23-20 loss to the previously winless Los Angeles Chargers. Adding Jones to the island of misfit QBs of Brissett and Joe Fagnano might help stabilize things for a proud Ravens franchise. Or it might just be time to ride with Brissett and look toward 2027.

How about a return to the NFC East, where Jones started his career in six seasons with the New York Giants? At 2-7 and with starter Jalen Hurts out indefinitely with blood clots, the Eagles' season is already lost. But suffering through the next eight weeks with Joe Milton III and Tanner McKee, neither of whom is seen as "quarterback of the future" material, might be untenable in front of Philly's famously prickly fans. Would Jones change Philly's long-term outlook? Probably not. But could he be a passable Hail Mary for first-year offensive coordinator Sean Mannion to call up? Perhaps.

#2945. White House replaces cyber director Sean Cairncross amid widespread tech outages

White House National Cyber Director Sean Cairncross is leaving his post, with Pentagon CIO Kirsten Davies set to serve as acting director after a week of high-profile U.S. technology outages.

The role, which advises the president on cybersecurity and coordinates U.S. cyber policy across federal agencies, has been handed to Davies, the Pentagon's chief information officer, on an interim basis. A permanent national cyber director must first be nominated by the president and then confirmed by the Senate.

Axios first reported Cairncross' exit, citing two White House officials who said President Donald Trump's chief of staff, Stephen Miller, pushed for his departure.

The White House declined to comment. However, a source familiar with the White House's thinking told CNBC that the President fully supported firing Sean Cairncross from his position because he lacked the strong cyber credentials to perform the job. Davies has the credentials to excel in the position and has been Senate-confirmed, the person added.

The Office of the National Cyber Director did not immediately respond to a CNBC request for comment.

It comes after a series of technology outages, including a nationwide aviation ground stop, a worldwide Microsoft Outlook outage and a reported blackout of the National Security Agency's classified communications network.

The White House has insisted that there are no indications of a coordinated cyberattack, state-sponsored activity or malign actor behind recent tech outages.

Davies addressed her responsibilities in the new position and thanked Miller and Trump for their confidence late Thursday.

"Our nation stands in the crossroad of analog, digital, and quantum technologies, our American way of living increasingly interconnected. Our opportunity is to dominate the cyber landscape for generations to come … but only if we modernize and drive resilience into the foundations our great nation runs on," she wrote on X.

Secretary of Defense Pete Hegseth and Secretary of State Marco Rubio both congratulated Davies on her appointment.

#2943. Yahoo Perfect 5: Nikola Jokić, Daniss Jenkins, Ja'Kobe Walter among my lineup options for Friday doubleheader

Yahoo Sports is giving fans another way to play fantasy basketball with Perfect 5, a free-to-play game featuring each week's NBA on Prime doubleheader. The rules are simple: pick five players, stay within a $15 budget and rack up fantasy points through scoring, rebounds, assists, defensive stats and 3s.

Friday's slate features the Raptors at Pistons (7:30 p.m. ET) and Rockets at Nuggets (10 p.m. ET), both games NBA Cup Group Play matchups. The injury report is doing most of the talking this week. Cade Cunningham, Alperen Şengün and Scottie Barnes are all questionable, and Detroit is already without Jalen Duren and Duncan Robinson. The Raptors won't have RJ Barrett and Collin Murray-Boyles.

Using Yahoo projections, each player's last five games, the betting lines and the latest injury news, I ran 20,000 simulations of the slate against a simulated Yahoo field built from this week's pick rates. I'm paying up for the best player on the board and filling in around him with $2 and $3 players whose roles just got bigger. Here's my lineup:

This group finished in the top 1% of the simulated field 16.9% of the time — the best rate of the 20,000 lineups I tested — and beat every entry in the field more often than any other build (1.04%). Only about 0.03% of the field has these exact five.

Jokić leads the slate at 54.00 projected fantasy points, more than three points clear of anyone else, on a line of 24.1 points, 10.7 rebounds, 8.9 assists, 1.7 steals and a block. I like the situational spot: Denver is a 3.5-point home favorite, and the 227.5 total is the highest on the slate. Yes, three-quarters of the field has him, but the matchup sets up well, too.

Şengün took a shot to the face in Wednesday's win over Indiana and didn't return. Ime Udoka said he has swelling in his right eye and a scratched cornea in his left. If Şengün sits, Houston's answer for Jokić is Steven Adams, and a village. Houston won the first meeting 110-100 in Houston on Nov. 9; this one is in Denver. Fading Jokić is a real option in a top-heavy contest like this (see the contrarian build below), but my sims say he's worth going chalk.

Jenkins is the best value on the board and I like him whether Cunningham plays or not. He's projected for 33.02 fantasy points at $3, ahead of several $4 players. He's been hot this week, too: 25 points, 5 rebounds and 5 assists in Tuesday's win over San Antonio, then 17 points and 9 assists as the starting point guard in Wednesday's win at New Orleans. Over his last five games, he's averaging about 36 fantasy points.

Cunningham sat Wednesday with right knee soreness on the second night of a back-to-back, and he's questionable again Friday. The expectation is he plays. But if he sits, Jenkins runs the offense and his outlook gets even better. That built-in insurance is why he showed up in the single best lineup in nearly 47% of my sims, more than any other player on the slate.

This is my leverage play. Walter is averaging 12.2 points, 2.5 3s and 1.3 steals, and he's projected for 27.15 fantasy points. What makes him interesting is everyone around him. Leonard and Barrett are out and Barnes is questionable after Toronto held him out of the back half of its back-to-back to manage his groin. Murray-Boyles is also in concussion protocol and won't play.

Walter dropped 25 points with seven 3s against the Knicks on Tuesday, then followed it with 18 points, 7 rebounds, 6 assists and 3 steals against Washington. Toronto has lost six straight. Someone has to take shots, and he's shown he will. At 4% picked, he's the piece that separates this lineup from the field.

Huerter was in the single best lineup in 45.3% of my simulations, second only to Jenkins. He's projected for 24.15 fantasy points at $2, and his role is growing with Detroit this banged-up. Robinson (left quad strain) will be re-evaluated in a week and Huerter started Wednesday, finishing with 11 points and 7 assists. He's hit double figures in five of his last six games, while averaging 33.1 minutes a night over that span. He's a cheap source of points, 3s, assists and the occasional block, and he doesn't need Cunningham to sit to pay off.

Johnson is the second-most popular pick in my lineup. He's projected for 23.13 fantasy points at $2, starting next to Jokić in the slate's highest-scoring game. He's averaging 11.4 points, 3.7 rebounds, 1.7 3s and 1.1 steals, a steady floor for the price. His recent body of work is meh (about 21 fantasy points a game over his last five), but he still landed in the optimal lineup about a third of the time. You don't get leverage from him, but you get it from Walter and Jenkins, and that's enough. I'm banking on a bounce-back from the 15.9 fantasy points he scored in 31 minutes versus Houston on Nov. 9.

Using Yahoo's projections, the highest-scoring possible lineup is Jokić, Cunningham, Huerter, Johnson and Alijah Martin at 161.63 fantasy points. Mine comes in just 0.18 points behind it.

On paper, it's the max. In the sims, it finished in the top 1% only 6.6% of the time, less than half the rate of my lineup. Two things hurt it. First, Cunningham's knee: a questionable tag means there's a real chance he sits. Second, Martin is a $1 punt projected under 10 points, so you're leaning on two players to carry the build. Swapping Cunningham and Martin for Jenkins and Walter gives up 0.18 projected points and removes most of that risk.

It reached the top 1% of the field 12.6% of the time and beat the entire field in 1.01% of sims, nearly matching my main lineup's win rate, and essentially nobody in the simulated field has these five.

#2942. Bitcoin sell-off: Crypto is failing the 'digital gold' test again as it sinks with stocks

Bitcoin's latest sell-off is reviving doubts that it can behave as the "digital gold" market hedge that crypto backers have long envisioned.

Coming into 2026, experts said that showing bitcoin could serve as a market hedge rather than a risk-on asset that moves up and down with growth stocks was an important test for the cryptocurrency.

Bitcoin was trading under $62,000 on Friday morning, down from an all-time high of more than $126,000 in early October last year. Its downward spiral in recent months and weeks has been precipitated by a confluence of factors. The heavy midterm elections losses for the Republicans, the Binance hack, and a world on edge about the security of all things technology — FAA ground stop, the outage of the National Security Agency's highly classified internal communications system, several online banking outages — have not helped the mood when it comes to putting trust in the digital world.

But the market itself may be the biggest factor. Investors are currently in sell everything mode, including tech stocks and gold — the Dow Jones Industrial Average and S&P 500 tumbled into correction territory on Friday, according to CNBC data. Bitcoin's recent price action has also tracked the move in the biggest tech IPOs, SpaceX and Anthropic. Taken together, the cryptocurrency is failing the test again when it comes to showing its worth as a market hedge during times of investor anxiety.

In the past month alone, bitcoin had dropped roughly $20,000, as of Friday morning, according to CNBC data.

Investors and financial management firms aren't viewing it as safe an investment as in the past, said Ryan Kirkley, co-founder and chief executive of Global Settlement Network, a Miami-based fintech that provides infrastructure for currency settlement, including crypto. "There's a bit more caution than there was a year ago."

What is behind bitcoin's fall, and what is in store for its future? Here is what experts say.

There have been many theories about how bitcoin would react based on different market and economic conditions. But historically, bitcoin has done best in low-rate environments and when lending opportunities are plentiful, said Juan Xavier Sanchez, head of wealth strategy at Activest Wealth Management in Aventura, Florida.

When rates are down and treasuries aren't paying good yields, people tend to be more risk-seeking for higher returns. "Bitcoin for many investors is in that bucket," Sanchez said.

Experts have noted that gold, which is now down over 35% from its early 2026 peak, is less attractive when cash accounts are paying 5%. Investments like gold and bitcoin offer no yield.

Bitcoin's rising correlation with the Nasdaq-100 since late September suggests the two are increasingly moving in tandem, according to data from Fairlead Strategies, a technical research firm. That's consistent with an environment of higher volatility in which correlations across risk assets tend to increase. However, the correlation remains below its early-2026 highs, the data show.

Bitcoin has certain characteristics of digital gold, in that it has a limited supply, no single owner and no CEO or board that can decide to issue more, said Anthony Georgiades, founder and general partner at Innovating Capital in New York, which invests in early-stage crypto, blockchain, digital infrastructure and deep tech companies.

But it's also been caught up in monetary policy since its inception. The last decade or so of monetary policy has been extremely rewarding to speculative risk-on assets, Georgiades said. However, in periods of volatility, those types of assets have experienced the most significant drawdowns and have been the most heavily punished, Georgiades said. That means that a shock on one side of the board that might be completely independent of bitcoin could trigger a large volatility event "given how intertwined digital assets have become in the overall financial system," he said.

Notably, bitcoin hasn't acted much like digital gold in more recent years, said Abe Sheikh, chief investment officer of Cordoba Advisory Partners in New York. In fact, it has fallen substantially during periods when the stock market has been falling. For example, from October 6 through November 18, the S&P 500 fell about 6.3%, and bitcoin fell around 27%. From January 21, 2025 through April 8, 2025, the S&P dropped around 17.4%, while bitcoin slid 27.8%, he said.

Democrats gaining control over both houses is viewed as a negative for crypto from a market perspective. The crypto industry spent more than $200 million to support crypto-friendly candidates, but its role had less of an impact on overall results than in the last election cycle. However, bitcoin's downward trend was already in the works, said Cyrus Amini, chief investment officer at Hyphen Wealth Management in Lafayette, California.

In September, the Clarity Act failed in the U.S. Senate, and while its outlook is now dim, according to many political and market experts, some form of crypto legislation is still considered a priority for many Democrats and Republicans, while the Trump-appointed heads of the Commodity Future Trading Commission and Securities and Exchange Commission continue to favor crypto in rulemaking.

This fall, investors also started to question the security of encryption technology, leading to more concerns over the security of their bitcoin holdings, said Kirkley of Global Settlement Network. The Binance hack in October muddied the waters further. And the recent stock market jitters and economic concerns piled on doubts, Kirkley said. The last few weeks have been especially rough with data security concerns from multiple fronts, he added.

Wall Street's embrace of bitcoin has been a big positive for the cryptocurrency. Kirkley noted that 20% of bitcoin's market price is coming from institutional investors, and that's a conservative estimate.

In addition, new research from digital assets company Galaxy shows that a record 40.5% of all 13F-reported spot bitcoin exchange-traded product holdings, and 11.2% of all the bitcoin in the funds, came from advisory and wealth planning firms, such as RIAs and brokerage platforms, as of the third quarter 13F filings with the SEC, which are required of firms with over $100 million in assets to report their holdings.

Much of this investment is through bitcoin spot ETFs like the iShares Bitcoin Trust ETF (IBIT) that track its price. These ETFs, first approved by the SEC in January 2024, were supposed to provide a reliable new source of institutional demand. But ETF ownership also makes bitcoin especially easy to sell.

Outflows from several of these funds have been substantial in recent months. IBIT, the largest of its peers, had six-month net outflows of $1.65 billion through Nov. 18 and one-month outflows of $1.37 billion, according to ETF Database. The second biggest, Fidelity Wise Origin Bitcoin Fund (FBTC) had one-month net outflows of $322.65 million and six-month net outflows of $771.86 million, according to ETF Database. Grayscale Bitcoin Mini Trust ETF (BTC) had one-month net outflows of $66.35 million, though it had inflows of $590.04 million over six months.

As of September 2026, public companies collectively hold close to 1.29 million bitcoins, which is more than 6% of the 21 million coins in existence, according to bitcoin.com.

Most public companies invest their corporate treasury portfolios in low-risk, highly liquid instruments such as money market funds, Treasury bonds and Treasury bills. That said, there are some well-known companies that have invested in bitcoin. SpaceX, for example, purchased 18,712 bitcoins for a total cost of $661 million, at an average price of $35,324 per coin. The company began accumulating bitcoin in early 2021 and held its position unchanged from mid-2024 until its IPO filing.

Digital Asset Treasury companies, known as DATs or DATCOs, have also played a role in bitcoin's volatility. DATs are publicly listed firms whose primary business strategy involves holding significant quantities of digital assets, such as bitcoin or ethereum, on their corporate balance sheets to drive shareholder value. Companies in this space include Strategy, Strive, and Twenty One Capital.

"DATs have exacerbated the problem," said Sheikh of Cordoba Advisory Partners. When bitcoin is rising, these companies borrow money in the debt market or raise capital to buy more bitcoin, which magnifies the upswings. They have also sold bitcoin to make their bond coupon payments. "It leads to enormous volatility on the upside and downside," Sheikh said.

As of mid-November, Strategy, the largest corporate holder of bitcoin, holds 850,849 BTC, according to a regulatory filing. That represents roughly 4% of bitcoin's total supply. The company acquired these holdings at a total cost of approximately $64.2 billion, with an average purchase price of roughly $75,452 per coin, according to the filing.

Bitcoin has been trending lower since the peak in October 2025. A trading range has taken hold and the bottom of the range is just below 60,000, said Katie Stockton, founder and managing partner of Fairlead Strategies. "It appears to be trying to find a footing," she said.

Oversold conditions on the heels of recent lows in June and July, in addition to long-term momentum, suggest bitcoin can support an upturn, she said. But that depends on whether the crypto can hold at a current range around $60,000, she said. "At the end of the day, bitcoin is exceptionally volatile. It's still an area that people might go to sell when they think that risk is higher in other areas."

By mid-morning Friday, the price of bitcoin had slipped below $60,000, its lowest mark since July. It was on pace for its seventh-straight weekly loss for the first time since May 2022, according to CNBC data.

#2941. Top Wall Street analysts like these 3 dividend stocks for consistent income

Democrats' midterm election win has put the artificial intelligence trade in question, as voters clearly expressed opposition to the rapid buildout of data centers. The stock market could remain volatile amid AI trade concerns, macro uncertainties, and geopolitical tensions.

In this scenario, investors can add dividend-paying stocks to their portfolios to earn steady income. However, the extensive universe of dividend stocks can make selection challenging. Top Wall Street analysts' recommendations can help investors shortlist attractive dividend stocks, as these experts assign ratings after in-depth financial analysis.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Viper Energy (VNOM) is a subsidiary of Diamondback and owns and acquires mineral and royalty interests, primarily in the Permian Basin. The company reported upbeat third-quarter earnings, supported by recent acquisitions and continued development activity from Diamondback and third-party operators. Viper announced a base cash dividend of 50 cents per share for Q3 2026, payable on Nov. 19. Based on the base and variable dividends announced over the past 12 months, VNOM's dividend yield stands at 5.7%.

Following the Q3 results, TD Cowen analyst Aaron Bilkoski reiterated a buy rating on Viper Energy stock with a price target of $61. The analyst noted that the company delivered solid Q3 results, with production, including oil and natural gas liquids, exceeding both TD Cowen and consensus estimates.

The 5-star analyst highlighted that the key update was the announcement of two royalty acquisitions for $809 million in cash, including deferred consideration, and $82 million of newly issued shares.

Given the recently announced base dividend hike, Bilkoski expects Viper to direct incremental free cash flow toward buybacks. While the acquisitions increase leverage, the analyst believes that Viper has enough capacity for opportunistic buybacks.

Bilkoski noted that Viper has historically delivered one of the highest production per share growth profiles in his royalty coverage, but still trades at a notable discount to the royalty peer average and the company's historical FCF yield range. "In our view, its above-average growth warrants a higher valuation," said the analyst.

Bilkoski ranks No. 525 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 57% of the time, delivering an average return of 12.80%. See Viper Energy Stock Buybacks on TipRanks.

Exxon Mobil (XOM) reported robust Q3 2026 profits, as its U.S. refineries boosted production to capitalize on the high diesel fuel prices sparked by the conflict in the Middle East. The oil and gas giant announced a quarterly dividend of $1.07 per share, payable on Dec. 10, reflecting a 3.9% increase. This marks Exxon's 44th consecutive year of dividend increases. At an annualized dividend of $4.28 per share, XOM offers a yield of 2.53%.

Impressed by the Q3 print, TD Cowen analyst Jason Gabelman reiterated a buy rating on Exxon stock and raised his price target to $193 from $180 to reflect higher downstream earnings, with the expectation of increased mid-cycle margins.

Gabelman noted that the strength in Exxon's refining business continues to offset Middle East-related headwinds. He added that the company increased its ownership in LNG projects, which should reduce its Middle East concentration over time from 75% to 50% and lower the likelihood of diversification-focused mergers and acquisitions. Beyond LNG, Gabelman expects Exxon's growth in the 2030s to come from Guyana, Kashagan, Permian, and possibly Venezuela.

Gabelman noted that Exxon announced a dividend hike of nearly 4% and plans to maintain buybacks at $20 billion for 2027. "The decision to maintain reduces the pro-cyclicality nature of the buyback," said the analyst.

Gabelman ranks No. 347 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 66% of the time, delivering an average return of 16.90%. See Exxon Mobil Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Williams (WMB), which owns and operates energy infrastructure to deliver natural gas, reported strong third-quarter results. The company announced a quarterly dividend of $0.525 per share, or $2.10 per share annualized, payable on Dec. 28. WMB offers a dividend yield of 3.09%.

In reaction to the Q3 2026 results, CIBC analyst Robert Catellier reaffirmed a buy rating on Williams stock with a price target of $82. The analyst noted that Q3 results were essentially in line with expectations and reflected continued strength in execution across WMB's infrastructure portfolio.

Catellier said that Williams' Q3 performance was driven by higher transmission, storage, and power generation revenues, as well as contributions from the Momentum Midstream acquisition.

He noted that Williams maintained its full-year EBITDA guidance of $8.3 billion to $8.5 billion. In fact, results are tracking toward the higher end of the guidance range, backed by contributions from recently completed projects and the Momentum Midstream deal. WMB continues to target long-term EBITDA compound annual growth rate of at least 11% through 2030.

Catellier expects WMB's long-term targets to be supported by incremental transmission and storage opportunities, including Transco's Southeast Supply Enhancement II project and an 84 MMcf/d expansion of the Garden Connector project.

Catellier ranks No. 667 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 66% of the time, delivering an average return of 9.60%. See Williams Technical Analysis on TipRanks.

#2940. Darius Acuff is showing flashes of NBA stardom, but he has one massive problem

Flip on a Sacramento Kings broadcast and you're likely to be inundated with graphics, statistical queries or fun facts framing Darius Acuff Jr. in an illustrious ensemble.

Names like Michael Jordan, LeBron James, Oscar Robertson and Luka Dončić are frequently mentioned alongside the rookie guard, who is averaging a robust 26.2 points, seven assists (3.9 turnovers) and 3.6 rebounds through the first 14 games of his career. It's an esteemed neighborhood for Acuff to reside in and certainly an encouraging omen as he progresses further into his NBA tenure.

Skeptics, however, will peek beyond the box score and point to Sacramento's 1-13 record, Acuff's relative scoring inefficiency (53.3% true shooting, 24th percentile, per Dunks & Threes) or his ball-dominant tendencies to downplay his raw production. They'll note how much of the Kings' 27th-ranked offense is channeled through him and posit his true impact pales in comparison to those All-Star-level statlines by citing a host of metrics and data.

Stretching back to his sensational freshman season at Arkansas a year ago, Acuff has divided the masses with his play. The first month of his rookie campaign has done little to assuage that polarization, a reality existing independent of anything he's responsible for but rather captures the cantankerous discourse enveloping the sports world more than ever these days.

Nonetheless, there is plenty to examine, praise and critique about Acuff's first 14 games. He's flashed loads of promise and indicated various areas of concern as the Kings presumably build around him moving forward. So, what about his skill-set may suggest future stardom and what may offer reasons for worry in an NBA ecosystem increasingly cruel and unforgiving to smaller guards?

Every star player needs a superpower. Some are more consequential or prominent than others but they all have one. Though not a star at this stage, Acuff's superpower is his ability to maintain a live dribble wherever and whenever he desires. That may sound rudimentary. There are a horde of guards who can haphazardly dribble the air out of the ball. But Acuff makes it immensely practical.

As the league continues to evolve and grow sharper fangs defensively while also upsizing its offensive hubs, dribbling — especially inside the arc or against pressure — is a bit of a lost art. So many players are unable to initiate offense while enduring full-court physicality or slalom through gap help. Acuff can.

He's already one of the most dextrous dribblers in the NBA, equipped to prime screens however necessary, vary the height of his dribble to best suit his intentions at any given moment, wind through treacherous limbs and preserve his handle in the face of point-of-attack pestering. If he wants to avoid stunting defenders or insulate the ball from pressure, he'll keep it low. If it's time to rise for a jumper or make a delivery, he pounds the rock to speed up its arrival into his shooting/passing pocket. The toggling between them is seamless, too.

Acuff is not solely a modern Curly Neal, he also touts an incredibly elastic upper body. Such a trait is evident in his dexterity — he can dribble and pass from a variety of arm and hand placements — but spans beyond it, too.

It empowers him to duck low underneath stiffer defenders or coil around them to find opportunistic angles. The dude gyrates, and it's integral to his offensive signature. Any potential stardom will hinge, in part, on his rare intersection of ball-handling and upper-body flexibility that fuels his playmaking and bucket-getting.

That superpower grants Acuff access to virtually any part of the court. The broader the scope a player can access on the court offensively, the better. It renders him a grander threat to defenses as a scorer and passer. Yet right now, Acuff is not an omnipresent threat. His wondrous ball-handling unlocks doors for him he cannot utilize.

He lacks the burst and vertical explosion to create separation as a driver or aptly elevate as a finisher, issues borne through insufficient lower-body strength that prevent him from generating force downhill or through the air.

He is not an imposing scorer around the rim, consistently reduced to attempts well below the basket or unable to discard help inside, even if momentum is in his corner. His handle and upper-body pliability mitigate some of these troubles but not all of them, which rear their head enough to disrupt his game and narrow the avenues where he's a daunting offensive conductor.

According to Cleaning the Glass, 17% of his field goal attempts occur at the rim (30th percentile among point guards) while he's converting just 58% of those attempts (35th percentile). Similarly, he's currently unable to overwhelm defenders and reliably draw fouls. Despite touting the league's sixth-highest usage rate (34.7%) and having taken the third-most shots, he's 39th in free throws attempted (68).

Just look how groundbound he is on these drives, missing both the pop to ease his finishes or the ferocity to challenge bigs inside:

A lean diet of shots at the rim and charity stripe makes for a tough life in building an efficient scoring repertoire. Until or if Acuff adds significant lower-body strength (and gradual increments could facilitate this, it's not all or nothing), the path toward efficient high-volume scorer is to embrace the 3-ball.

He's already doing so off the bounce, launching the league's third-most pull-up triples per game at 6.9. Although he's only netting 32% of those, it's clearly a shot he's hunting and cozying up with, largely against drop coverage around Domantas Sabonis' bludgeoning screens. But he'll also dance into them over switches and still hoist when a trailing point-of-attack defender is re-entering the play.

Thirty-two percent cannot suffice long-term but it's far from a disastrous launching pad. As he approaches his prime, it's entirely plausible the newly minted 20-year-old levels up as a shotmaker to better suit the volume with which he's evidently found comfort.

If Acuff ascends to a requisite level as a long-range pull-up shooter that warps defenses into dissuading those looks, it should benefit his interior pursuits. Deep drop coverage is an arduous tactic for him to attack inside because of aforementioned physical limitations. But seeing more bigs switch or play around the level — and away from the paint — could allow him to weaponize his handle and flexibility into drives without the fear of a rim protector lurking.

Ideally, the Kings will also add another bona fide creator alongside Acuff, whose off-ball game has yet to be spotlighted much because of the rickety ball-handling carousel surrounding him. Among starting guards, his 49.8% on-ball rate ranks third behind Jalen Brunson and Andrew Nembhard, the latter of whom has played much of the year without Tyrese Haliburton (per databallr).

In the rare moments he's been afforded to operate off the ball, his instincts locating space, traversing screens or curling into dribble handoffs lend credence to the notion his sprawling ball dominance is a product of Sacramento's cumbersome infrastructure more than his individual nature.

Even in the small sample he's shared the floor with Malik Monk — who's struggled mightily this year but is still the Kings' most viable non-Acuff creator as someone who can dribble, initiate possessions and operate a ball-screen — Acuff's efficiency spikes without suppressing his volume. Whether it's spotting up, being deployed as a screener or piercing a tilted defense, he's yielded the rewards of defenses fixating on someone else.

Through 14 games, Acuff is shooting 41.7% (15-of-36) on catch-and-shoot threes. An environment promoting more of those chances and opportunities for his quick-witted, malleable decision-making — Acuff adapts his shooting and passing processes on the fly quite well — would simplify his responsibilities without dimming his light.

That doesn't mean Acuff is ill-suited for an on-ball role, he's exhibited reasons to believe in him there. But the extent to which Sacramento relies upon him for it seems detrimental to himself and the collective offense.

Between occasional minutes next to Monk and his screening/handoff camaraderie with Sabonis, the Kings do present a few helping hands for their rookie point guard. That all evaporates defensively, though. There's hardly any safety net.

They're a horrendous rim protection team; opponents shoot a league-high 75.6% at the basket against them, per Cleaning the Glass. They don't have established point-of-attack stoppers or slippery screen navigators. They switch the majority of actions 1-4 because most of their rotation players cannot fight around picks and that makes Acuff a moving target for opposing offenses.

Despite a litany of debilitating factors, Acuff does himself no favors either. He is, thus far, a very, very poor defender. He applies scant physicality on the ball, gets trapped in screens like a never-ending maze, hardly ever forces turnovers or grabs rebounds and is often ill-positioned off the ball without the tools to recover punctually.

Habits like these gleaned from the eye test are reinforced by various datapoints. Per Cleaning the Glass, with Acuff on the floor, opponents take 6.9% more of their shots at the rim, log a 2.1% higher offensive rebounding rate and turn the ball over 3.6% less of the time. His STOP% (steals, blocks recovered, offensive fouls drawn per 100 possessions) is 1.6 and ranks in the 6th percentile, according to databallr.

Playing for the league's 30th-ranked defense exacerbates the magnitude of Acuff's woes but he is culpable for the vast majority of his shortcomings and must improve in the coming years. He does not make offenses feel his presence and appears to simply drift through possessions instead of committing to sustainable defensive efforts.

As defensive-minded players blossom into loftier offensive duties, we sometimes see them regress defensively. Maybe, through an optimistic lens, the opposite can unfold for Acuff. He shoulders a titanic offensive load and has displayed the capacity to function off the ball in a lower-stress role.

For that to ignite meaningful defensive growth, though, Acuff probably has to infuse substantial lower-body strength. It'll make him sturdier and more liable to anchor against contact, which he's mostly shied away from altogether, and accelerate his lateral mobility, where he concedes breakdowns because he cannot slide quickly enough to cut off ball-handlers.

Achieving this could address glaring limitations on both sides of the ball and amplify his superpowers — the very ones propelling him into graphics of glory alongside Hall of Famers. Maybe someday, Acuff, too, will be the sort of complimentary company rookie guards hope to join if they burst onto the NBA scene.

#2937. Bill Simmons found a lowlight. Jalen Green found the reply button

The internet is forever. The internet never forgets.

One of the pitfalls of social media is that we often utilize it to express opinions in a heightened emotional state. Typically, you can fire off a tweet, think better of it, and delete it before many people even notice. That luxury diminishes considerably when you have a sizable following, as is the case with Phoenix Suns guard Jalen Green, who has over 180,000 followers on Twitter. Sure, it's not Kevin Durant territory. Durant has 27.6 million followers, and as we know all too well here in Phoenix, very little of his social media activity goes unnoticed.

An interesting exchange occurred today after Bill Simmons tweeted the following. The accompanying video features Jalen Green standing wide open for several seconds before missing a three-pointer against the Sacramento Kings during the Suns' victory on Wednesday night.

I'm sure Bill Simmons was being somewhat tongue-in-cheek when he captioned the video, "This is the official video of the 2026–27 trade deadline."

Was it a great moment for Jalen Green? No. But in the same breath, it would suggest that Simmons didn't watch the basketball game. Green had a productive night, scoring 18 points to go along with 7 assists and 7 rebounds. The miss Simmons chose to highlight was one of only three Green had from beyond the arc, as he went 4-of-7 from downtown. His defensive intensity, which is becoming increasingly apparent as he continues to find his role within the Phoenix Suns, was also on full display Wednesday night.

Given the non-existent defense Zach LaVine played on the play and the drop coverage trade machine All-Star Domantas Sabonis was playing, I see the point Simmons was making. Green's name has been in the hopper since Rich Paul decided Phoenix would be the next destination for a disgruntled Dejounte Murray. And LaVine? His name is always in trade machines as people believe that adding a score-first, defense-last player who was incorrectly awarded a dunk contest a decade ago can be a solution. The video highlighted two players with large contracts who have underdelivered and personify the methodology of shuffling decks on the Titanic.

Ultimately, emotions got the best of Green, who responded with the following:

The tweet was soon deleted, and quite honestly, I don't know exactly what it means. With phraseology like that, you could interpret it a number of different ways.

What's interesting is that as recently as yesterday, Jalen Green acknowledged that he's aware of the noise surrounding him.

"I mean, I see a little bit (of the reports)," Green stated. "This is people's jobs. They think they're doing something with the internet and feel like they got to post things like this. I've seen a couple of things, but I don't really care. I've already been traded once. I'm focused on getting better and winning games."

It's one thing to hear the noise locally from those who watch you play every night. It's another when a prominent national voice cherry-picks one moment from an otherwise productive performance and uses it as the proverbial promotional video for the trade machine that will inevitably dominate NBA discourse over the next few months. That's more than noise. That's an annoyance.

Perhaps the best response Green can provide moving forward is more of what we witnessed Wednesday night. He's beginning to demonstrate that his value to this team extends beyond his ability to score, and that's a development worth paying attention to. Unfortunately, a missed three-pointer generates considerably more engagement than a productive night of facilitating and defending. Such is the nature of NBA discourse in the social media age, where context is often the first casualty of a good tweet.

#2934. Bitcoin's regulation bull case is played out. What could drive crypto gains moving forward

Crypto investors are hunting for the next positive catalyst after U.S. crypto regulation — a longtime market driver — fizzled in 2026.

Bitcoin traded around $61,500 on Friday, a more than 50% decline from its all-time high above $126,000 reached in October 2025. The broader digital asset market has struggled after the Clarity Act, a crypto market structure bill, failed to advance in the Senate in September.

Investors now see Wall Street's embrace of blockchain technology — particularly tokenization — as the next trend to give bitcoin a boost.

"Every single traditional firm is now looking at digital assets," BitGo CEO Mike Belshe told CNBC last week in an exclusive interview from the fintech-focused Cantor Convergence conference in Miami Beach. That includes BlackRock, the world's largest asset manager.

He pointed to statements from the asset management giant's CEO, Larry Fink, who said the next major evolution in global finance is the creation of digital representations of every stock, bond, fund and real-world asset onto a shared blockchain ledger.

"I think the biggest catalyst we're seeing right now is actually very much in motion and it's happening, which is capital markets are moving onchain," eToro CEO Yoni Assia told CNBC in an exclusive interview from the conference. He pointed to the SpaceX IPO as an example, which had derivatives trading on eToro before the IPO.

"First day of trading, we distributed it to our retail clients, and it started trading 24/7, and that's all because capital markets are now copying crypto markets and are sitting on the same rails," Assia said. "Crypto markets have always been 24/7, they never sleep, and now what we're seeing is that capital markets are copying crypto markets using the same technology so stocks are moving onchain, they're being tokenized."

Assia said that supportive regulation in the U.S. from agencies, including the Securities and Exchange Commission and the Commodity Futures Trading Commission, is also helping adoption of crypto.

He pointed to the CFTC earlier this month clearing the way for crypto-style perpetual futures tied to individual stocks to trade on regulated U.S. exchanges for the first time. Assia also highlighted the SEC's move to issue an order in September, creating a regulatory pathway for certain trading venues to trade tokenized representations of publicly traded U.S. stocks.

"Blockchain technology is now starting to power what's happening in capital markets," Assia told CNBC. "That is the one of the biggest changes we're going to see in capital markets for many, many years."

"We're talking about hundreds of trillions of dollars that are going to be tokenized and move onchain and at the same time, I do believe that will support prices of bitcoin and other blockchains as well," he continued.

The industry is also watching whether inflation will ease and geopolitical tensions will subside in the new year, which could lower broader macro risk currently hampering the industry.

Last month, Strategy's Michael Saylor said he thinks bitcoin's next bull market is already underway.

ARK Invest Chief Investment Officer Cathie Wood told CNBC the investment management firm's $1.5 million bull case price target for bitcoin by 2030 still stands. She also said that it wouldn't surprise her if bitcoin reached its all-time high again before the end of 2027.

"Our macro forecast has inflation breaking down considerably," Wood told CNBC in an exclusive interview at Cantor Convergence. "I think the elections have increased our confidence there because the war [in Iran], one way or the other, is going to end."

She added that "bitcoin has weathered so much bad news," including the $6.3 billion Binance hack and the "distraction" of AI which, "many people would say is sucking the oxygen away from bitcoin."

The maturation of the artificial intelligence trade could provide an opportunity for bitcoin to find new inflows.

Wood said that ARK Invest believes bitcoin has found its low and stressed that cybersecurity is "top of mind right now," which could provide yet another opportunity for crypto markets to rebound.

"I think if the Bitcoin and crypto world coalesces and makes investors feel that they've got their arms around security and actually understand that there might be more security issues in the traditional financial system, I think we're going to see a wave of support," Wood said.

#2932. SpaceX’s subsidiary Cursor tells lawmakers it's considering phasing out Chinese AI models

SpaceX's artificial-intelligence coding platform, Cursor, is considering moving away from using of Chinese AI models due to "geopolitical risk," CNBC has learned.

Cursor, which SpaceX acquired at an implied $60 billion equity value in August, had built its coding model Composer 2 on top of Chinese lab Moonshot AI's Kimi K2.5.

It's among the U.S. firms that have adopted Chinese models, whose usage has spiked across the globe this year, worrying lawmakers in Washington. The battle for AI supremacy between the U.S. and China has reached new heights in 2026 as Chinese models have improved.

But now Cursor plans to "explore phasing out PRC [People's Republic of China]-developed open-weight models because of geopolitical risk," according to a report produced by the House Committee on Homeland Security and the House Select Committee on China, shared exclusively with CNBC ahead of its publication on Friday.

The report, which follows a months-long investigation into the adoption of Chinese AI models into the American tech stack, said Cursor "informed the Committees" of those plans. Airbnb and DoorDash also engaged with the investigation about their use of Chinese AI models.

SpaceX announced it entered into a formal agreement to buy Cursor in June; the deal was completed in August. SpaceX is developing the Grok family of models, which Cursor has been involved in training.

Cursor, SpaceX and DoorDash did not respond to a CNBC request for comment. Airbnb declined to comment. The U.S. Commerce Department's Bureau of Industry and Security did not immediately respond to a request for comment.

The report found that cost, customization and near-frontier performance of Chinese AI models were driving adoption, with the U.S. lacking a competitive open-weight alternative at "price and performance the market demands."

"Beijing is now seeking to turn those technological gains into global market share and long-term dependence," the report says. China's embassy in the U.K. did not respond to a request for comment. China's Foreign Ministry did not immediately respond.

It also sets out recommendations to address the issues it has identified.

Those include launching a "national American open-weight initiative" that combines public computing power, shared evaluation infrastructure and "lawful access" to American frontier models for training.

The report also says an order, made public in October, that banned DeepSeek from federal civilian agency systems, should be built on, by prohibiting the use of "PRC-developed AI models" in federal information systems and in performance of federal contracts.

Separately, Washington is also concerned about Chinese companies accessing Nvidia chips remotely, via overseas data centers, and gaining ground by using "distillation," where new models use older, more established ones to help train them.

The U.S. Commerce Department's Bureau of Industry and Security, or BIS, announced on Monday an interim final rule designed to prevent Chinese companies' remote access to AI chips.

#2930. ESPN urged to dump Troy Aikman before the Super Bowl

After years of promoting the fact that Joe Buck and Troy Aikman will call the first Super Bowl on ESPN, they're struggling to get across the finish line.

For a couple of weeks, it seemed like the tension between Buck and Aikman was beginning to subside. The headlines around their well-documented blowup over differing contract situations had faded, and the Monday Night Football duo continued to produce high-quality broadcasts. ESPN president Jimmy Pitaro even acknowledged he was disappointed in the coverage around Buck and Aikman ahead of this season's Super Bowl, but was confident in their ability to remain professional.

Everything changed Thursday morning, however, when Aikman made arguably the biggest headline by flippantly accusing Buck of "always wanting a one-man booth" during his weekly radio appearance with The Musers on 96.7 The Ticket in Dallas. And while everyone was already coming around to the fact that the relationship between Buck and Aikman would end after this season, Meadowlark Media's David Samson believes it needs to end now.

"Troy Aikman has gotten to the 'I don't give an eff stage' of his career with ESPN because he keeps doing these things. And here's the problem, Jimmy Pitaro has to end this," Samson insisted. "I don't know whether he ends it today, tomorrow, next week or next month. But ESPN cannot have Joe Buck and Troy Aikman call the Super Bowl. They have invested too much money to have any distraction that they know about entering into the lead-up to the Super Bowl.

"There's always going to be something that happens. But if I am Jimmy Pitaro, I am making sure that I find someone to call the Super Bowl right now, and that I start the process. So that the audience, the media, the gossipers, that we get it all out of our system. We deal with the fact that Aikman is gone from Monday Night Football, and we deal with it now. So by the time the playoffs and the Super Bowl comes, it's no longer part of the conversation."

Boomer Esiason floated the possibility that Aikman might not call the Super Bowl when initial news broke around the current turmoil inside the Monday Night Football booth. And it seemed far-fetched at the time, but Esiason was speaking from experience. One month later, the idea of Aikman not making it to the Super Bowl is seeming a little more real.

Chances are Aikman calls Super Bowl LXI alongside Buck. No one will look good if this 25-year partnership goes up in flames to the point where they have to break up during the season. But two or three months ago, no one would have predicted the Buck-Aikman relationship would look as damaged as it does today.

If ESPN wants the most attention on their Super Bowl broadcast and the best chance at having a great call, then they need to keep Buck and Aikman together for the next few months. If ESPN wants to regain control of the situation, then they should boot Aikman from the booth now. But are they really concerned this will become something worse than tabloid headlines? Aikman took a pointed shot at Buck this week. But it wasn't necessarily a sign that Aikman is preparing to turn the Super Bowl stage into open season on ESPN.

The post David Samson believes ESPN needs to cut ties with Troy Aikman before Super Bowl appeared first on Awful Announcing.

#2928. U.S.-China trade, Taiwan and tech tensions remain unresolved as Trump returns from Asia

SHENZHEN, China — Even as Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation leaders gathered for high-profile photos at the summit's close, key issues from Nvidia to Taiwan remained unaddressed.

"The intrigue is around not what was said, but what remains unresolved," said Gary Dvorchak, managing director at Blueshirt.

"Taiwan remains a major source of strategic uncertainty. North Korea was notably absent from the trilateral nuclear declaration," he said. "Southeast Asia, which is increasingly central to global manufacturing and investment, was overshadowed by major-power diplomacy."

President Donald Trump did not meet with North Korea's leader Kim Jong Un, despite speculation.

Taiwan, one of the 21 member economies of APEC, sent Acer Group founder and honorary chairman Stan Shih to Shenzhen. But the self-governed island, which Beijing claims is part of its territory, did not feature in any significant way during the event. Taiwan is set to hold key elections for Taipei mayor and other posts on Nov. 28.

Instead, the highlight of this week's APEC summit in Shenzhen was a trilateral meeting Thursday between Trump, Chinese President Xi Jinping and Russian President Vladimir Putin. The three leaders, meeting together for the first time, reaffirmed interests in reducing nuclear risks and plans to increase oil production.

"The optics are impressive, but there's not much happening," Ian Bremmer, president and founder of Eurasia Group, said Friday of the trilateral on CNBC's "Squawk Box Asia."

Resolving the years-long Ukraine war would require involvement from President Volodymyr Zelenskyy and European allies, Bremmer said.

Ahead of the trilateral, Trump and Xi also held their third bilateral summit of the year. Xi greeted Trump at the airport late Wednesday, reciprocating the hospitality shown in September when the Chinese leader visited Washington.

But little was shared publicly on whether the two countries agreed to reduce tariffs further, allow rare earths to keep flowing beyond a January deadline, or address tech competition.

Recent reports tying Jensen Huang to an alleged Chinese spy led to the executive's resignation as Nvidia CEO. It also raised questions in Congress about the effectiveness of U.S. efforts to restrict China's access to advanced chips used to train artificial intelligence.

Trump indicated to Fox News that Nvidia came up briefly in his talks with Xi. U.S. officials last week said Trump did not plan to raise the matter, while noting Tesla and SpaceX CEO Elon Musk would accompany the U.S. president.

No other executive was named. Other American business leaders, including Visa CEO Ryan McInerney, who also chairs the U.S.-China Business Council, joined the APEC CEO Summit and was seen at a welcome banquet Wednesday evening.

However, Musk did not appear with Trump at major APEC-related events. CNBC was present for the opening portions of a banquet Wednesday and the Economic Leaders' Meeting Thursday and did not see Musk.

In fact, Musk flew to Shanghai Thursday morning to visit Tesla's factory there, a company executive said on Chinese social media. Musk also showed up in a social media video with Ren Hongbin, chairman of the China Council for the Promotion of International Trade.

Musk addressed Wednesday's DealBook Summit in New York via video conference, having previously said on X that he would be joining from China.

Tesla and the White House did not immediately respond to CNBC's requests for comment.

"We still don't have a durable resolution of the U.S.-China trade dispute," Dvorchak said, adding: "Business needs certainty, not just ceremony."

Trump visited Shenzhen after briefly traveling to the Philippines on Tuesday for a meeting with the leaders of Australia, India and Japan as part of the Quad grouping happening on the sidelines of the ASEAN Summit. Trump also met the prime minister of Thailand, Anutin Charnvirakul.

Topics of discussion included U.S. cooperation with the region, trade and critical minerals, U.S. Ambassador to ASEAN Kevin Kim told reporters Wednesday. He declined to comment on whether China's influence in the region was addressed.

Kim emphasized it was the second year in a row that Trump visited Southeast Asia, and noted the region "will be at the heart of SI [artificial intelligence] going forward," making it important that American technology is "getting into the right hands" through a regulatory framework the president is building.

Speaking to reporters a day later in Shenzhen, Japanese Prime Minister Sanae Takaichi also highlighted the ASEAN and Quad events over APEC. "The major outcome of this diplomatic week was the Quad Summit," she said, noting via official English translation how the four leaders expressed "strong opposition to the unilateral approach to change the status quo by force or coercion ... [as well as] arbitrary export control."

Takaichi, the first Japanese leader in seven years to visit China, received a cool reception upon her arrival in Shenzhen for the APEC meeting.

Tensions between Japan and China have escalated since Takaichi drew Beijing's ire in Nov. 2025 when she said that a Chinese attack on Taiwan could draw a Japanese military response.

Takaichi said Thursday that Japan's position on Taiwan remained unchanged as stated in the 1972 Japan-China Communiqué.

The communique said Japan "understands and respects" the People's Republic of China's position on Taiwan, but does not explicitly acknowledge Taiwan as part of the PRC.

— CNBC's Anniek Bao and Lim Hui Jie contributed to this report.

#2926. Bitcoin suffered identical sell-off pattern ahead of Anthropic, SpaceX IPOs — what happens next?

Bitcoin has suffered nearly identical sell-offs ahead of two of the biggest public offerings this year, leaving investors wondering if competing demand for investor capital could be weighing on the cryptocurrency and perhaps delaying its recovery.

The price of the bitcoin dropped 22.7% in the 30 days before Anthropic's IPO and fell 22.3% in the month leading up to SpaceX's June 12 listing. Ether declined 25.3% in the 30 days preceding Anthropic's debut, and 28% in the same window ahead of SpaceX.

"Liquidity events can elevate correlations across asset classes quickly," Matthew Sheffield, chief investment officer at the ether treasury firm Sharplink, told CNBC. "The first 15 min of [Thursday's] Anthropic IPO put that on full display. Tech stocks, value stocks, oil, gold, crypto all sold off as traders sold assets to buy shares in the secondary market."

"Stores of value function as such over long horizons, but in the short term are still liquidity driven," Sheffield said. "We saw this in early Covid when the market fled to cash. Stock, bonds, commodities ... it didn't matter, you were being sold for cash."

While investors appear to be rotating capital out of crypto to make room for history-making IPO investments, that can't be said with precise certainty. Crypto has also been under pressure from ETF outflows, leverage, macro uncertainty and broader risk-off sentiment.

Still, the post-IPO price action may offer a data point in understanding where bitcoin's price goes next. With Anthropic now public, investors are watching to see whether bitcoin and ether find a floor, and if institutional demand returns.

"[After] the SpaceX IPO, bitcoin didn't truly find its final bottom until after the IPO was completed, and it didn't actually reverse the downtrend until 70 days later," said Matt Cole, CEO and chairman of Strive Asset Management, a bitcoin treasury firm. "My view is that [for] bitcoin, the most likely scenario is that it chops around here."

The expiration of IPO allocation restrictions can influence the timing of capital flows into digital assets, Sheffield said.

"Post SpaceX we saw the wealth effect in full force," Sheffield said. "Many brokers make traders wait 15-30 days before they are allowed to flip shares they received as allocations. … If you went long ETH versus SpaceX on the day of the IPO, on day 31 was when you would have become profitable."

Bitcoin's next critical test is between $60,000 and $62,000, according to Nicolai Sondergaard, a research analyst at Nansen. On Friday, it was sitting at about $62,000.

As of Friday, bitcoin was hovering at the upper end of a critical $60,000 to $62,000 support range. Its next move could determine whether it can stabilize or if it will face another leg lower and revisit its July low of $57,737.35.

Throughout November, bitcoin has been struggling from a combination of dollar strength, institutional selling, and ongoing leveraged bitcoin liquidations. The declines began in late October, as concerns over artificial intelligence valuations and spending fueled a broad tech sell-off. They were compounded by the $6.3 billion Binance hack and weakening institutional ETF demand.

Bitcoin is in the early stages of forming a bottom, according to Cole. Retail fear is elevated amid recent outages, quantum concerns and other short-term uncertainties, but bitcoin should continue to benefit from macro forces like rising debt and weakening fiat currencies, which should drive price higher, he said.

"Capital markets are open, … retail fears are high, and when you combine those things it's much more likely to be a dip-buying opportunity across the board, including bitcoin, than the start of a great financial crisis," said Cole. "It'll likely age as a volatile time to consider allocating to risk, not the opposite."

"The winter for bitcoin is basically at the end, but after winter does not come summer, it comes early spring," he added. "Anytime anyone's been around early spring, what you notice is it gets really cold still, it still rains – it's not necessarily great weather, and I think that's the environment we're in for bitcoin."

#2923. Pick Six: New York Jets at Los Angeles Chargers

The New York Jets are, embarrassingly, a three-point underdog on Sunday against a team who didn't earn their first win until less than a week ago. The Los Angeles Chargers will be looking to build on their 23-20 win over the Ravens, and the Jets are making the long trip out west to take them on.

Here are six storylines to watch as New York seeks to inject some positivity into their season:

Last week, we discussed the Jets' varying options for their slot cornerback position and they eventually went with one of our suggestions, which was to primarily use Minkah Fitzpatrick there in nickel packages, bringing Malachi Moore off the bench to fill in at safety.

The Jets potentially face a similar dilemma in the slot this week, albeit on offense. Both Omar Cooper and Isaiah Williams have been unable to practice, so if neither can go on Sunday, the Jets will have to make a change.

One option could be to go with the guys currently on the roster. You could reactivate Jelani Woods and Tim Patrick and either run more multiple tight end packages, or use players like Garrett Wilson there. Another option could be to bring up Sterling Shepard or Jamaal Pritchett from the practice squad.

The job share at running back remains a work in progress

The Jets are still trying to figure out how to get the best out of Braelon Allen and Breece Hall now that both are healthy at the same time. Allen's performances while Hall was out proved that he can benefit from a bigger workload. However, Hall brings different things to the table in the passing game, so the Jets will want him on the field more often than not.

Last week was interesting because they employed Hall more in the passing game in the first half with Allen getting more carries. At the half, Allen had six carries and Hall had just two. However, after halftime, Allen carried the ball just twice and Hall had nine carries. It's possible the Jets' thinking was that Hall might produce better after halftime if he was fresh and the defense was tired. In the end, the pair had similar rushing numbers, but Hall produced more as a receiver.

You can probably expect the Jets to keep working on this with one option being flipping last week's gameplan on its head and using Hall early, then keeping Allen fresh to be the hammer in the second half. The Chargers' run defense did a great job against the Ravens, holding them to 16 yards on 15 carries, so it might not be easy to get a running game established.

The Chargers' leading tackler is linebacker Daiyan Henley, but he's been managing a knee issue all season and has been missing practice this week.

Henley, who is also second on the team in defensive snaps played, has been exploited in coverage at times this year, but is still a key contributor to the Chargers' run defense and tends to be the guy who wears the defensive headset.

If Henley can't go, the next man up would be Marlowe Wax, who has played under 100 defensive snaps in his career.

Chargers quarterback Justin Herbert has been struggling in the passing game. A large part of that is that the Chargers have struggled to protect him. Herbert has been sacked 33 times so far this season; more than every NFL quarterback apart from Deshaun Watson. Only two of their starting offensive linemen have started all nine games, which hasn't helped matters.

Last week, the Jets increased Will McDonald's workload as they had promised to, but it meant that Kingsley Enagbare only got to rush the passer a season-low 15 times. With Joseph Ossai now also back in the rotation, sustaining McDonald's level of opportunities might prove tricky. He did respond with a sack last week, though.

The Jets's run defense continues to struggle with two of their starting interior defensive linemen on injured reserve, although one – David Onyemata – was activated for practice this week, so is eligible to return.

New York gave up over 200 yards to the Bills, including a back-breaking 74-yard touchdown in the fourth quarter. The damage could have been even worse had it not been for the efforts of Harrison Phillips, though, as he stopped 10 runs for a gain of four or less.

Los Angeles has the NFL's worst scoring offense but they actually boast a top-10 running game and are coming off a game against the Ravens where Omarion Hampton rushed for over 100 yards for the first time all year. In addition, Herbert gave them a boost with a season-high 51 rushing yards, including his first touchdown.

When the Jets won games last year, a feature of these performances was good production in the return game but that hasn't been happening in 2026. If Williams is out this week, maybe that wouldn't be the worst thing, because he's been much less productive than last season.

In last week's game, the Jets used Isaiah Davis on kickoffs and he did quite well, having always put up solid numbers in the past. If Williams is out, then they may elevate someone like Pritchett to return punts, although the depth chart on the team's official website lists Davis as the backup punt returner too.

The bad news is that the Chargers' coverage numbers are pretty solid, led by ex-Jets linebacker Del'Shawn Phillips who was the NFL's leader in special teams tackles last season. On kickoffs, Cameron Dicker has allowed the third lowest return average in the NFL and their punter JK Scott is the number one graded punter according to Pro Football Focus rankings.

#2922. Jensen Huang's — and Nvidia's — legal exposure balloons

The still-unraveling scandal of former Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang's extramarital affairs and the tech giant's potential role in its advanced semiconductor chips ending up in China present a legal minefield for Huang and Nvidia.

Federal and state prosecutors and Nvidia employees may target Huang and the company in court, where some shareholders have already taken action.

That's on top of a rapidly growing pile of probes by Republicans who control Congress now and Democrats who will have majorities in both the House and Senate come January.

"It's not going to get any better for Jensen Huang; it's going to get far worse," said Salvatore Graziano, a New York securities litigation attorney at Bernstein, Litowitz, Berger & Grossmann. His firm is looking at shareholder lawsuit opportunities against Nvidia and Huang.

[Huang] "faces the Department of Justice, the Securities and Exchange Commission and private litigation," Graziano said. "The criminal exposure is very serious. The SEC could bar him from being an officer. And the potential liability [from shareholder] lawsuits can be very large."

Huang's and Nvidia's legal exposure stems from three issues that led up to his shock resignation Nov. 12.

There's his alleged affair with suspected Chinese spy Alice Huang, who worked for Megaspeed, a Singapore-based company under investigation for diverting advanced Nvidia semiconductor chips to China in violation of U.S. export rules. That, in turn, has raised questions about whether Huang and Nvidia were aware of any diversions.

There are Huang's two other alleged affairs with Nvidia employees.

And there is the sharp drop in the company's stock price in the wake of the scandal. Nvidia's share price has fallen 19% since Nov. 8, when Huang sent a company-wide apology acknowledging extramarital affairs.

Huang last week retained the law firm Paul, Weiss and its attorneys Melinda Haag and Walter Brown to represent him. Haag is a former U.S. attorney in San Francisco, and Brown is also a former federal prosecutor.

Huang's spokesman at Trident, a crisis communications firm, declined to comment when asked if Haag and Brown are representing him in any potential criminal case and other questions about his legal exposure.

Nvidia did not respond to a request for comment. Last week, Nvidia retained the law firm Wachtell, Lipton, Rosen & Katz to conduct an independent investigation into Huang allegedly being targeted by a spy, as U.S. intelligence reportedly has concluded.

The civil liability Huang and Nvidia could face is underscored by a hearing Graziano, the securities attorney, attended Tuesday in U.S. District Court in San Francisco. A judge at the hearing approved a $175 million settlement from Seagate Technology Holdings to resolve a shareholder lawsuit alleging it concealed sales of hard-disk drives to Huawei in China in violation of export bans.

The Seagate settlement came three years after the Commerce Department's Bureau of Industry and Security — which is investigating Megaspeed's business with Nvidia — imposed a $300 million penalty on Seagate for its sales to Huawei.

Graziano said his law firm is "actively looking at securities law liability for Nvidia, Jensen Huang and other senior executives in the company given all the recent developments regarding their [potential] knowledge and facilitation of illegal chip sales to Chinese entities."

There is already a pending class-action shareholder lawsuit in San Francisco federal court against Nvidia and Huang by the Rosen Law Firm, for allegedly misleading and false statements related to Megaspeed and Alice Huang.

"Several hundreds of investors have contacted us and expressed an interest to join the case — investors of all walks of life, from mom and pops to investors with very large losses," Rosen attorney Phil Kim told CNBC in an email.

Katie Sweeten, a former federal prosecutor in the DOJ's National Security Division, said the question of "who knew what and when, and what was [Huang's] role" is potentially the biggest risk for Nvidia and him.

"Now DOJ can leverage their criminal authorities," Sweeten, a partner at Scale LLP, told CNBC's Kelly Evans on "The Exchange."

"They can bring grand jury subpoenas, search warrants for emails, bank accounts, personal devices, computers, things like that," Sweeten said.

While it is "not illegal to, you know, sleep with a spy ... it is illegal to pass certain types of information to a counterintelligence officer," she said.

Sweeten also pointed to potential export control violations, which "could be very serious."

She noted the DOJ can indict a company as well as its officials if there is evidence of criminality. Export control violations carry a maximum possible criminal sentence of 20 years in prison and "really large fines," she said.

"You can't send the company to prison, but they can face fines, civil penalties, debarment — where you can't export items down the line," she said.

One example of that came in July 2025, when the DOJ obtained a guilty plea and an agreement to pay more than $140 million from Cadence Design Systems for violating the Export Control Reform Act by unlawfully exporting semiconductor design tools to China's National University of Defense Technology, a school led by China's Central Military Commission.

Another potential legal risk for Huang comes from his planned appearances before Congress.

Huang is scheduled to give a closed-door interview Dec. 1 to three House committees. Senate banking committee Chair Tim Scott, R-S.C., wants Huang's testimony before that panel to be public, and told CNBC Tuesday he hopes to have that happen "before Christmas."

Sweeten said Huang "has to be very careful" about what he says there because of the risk of being criminally charged with lying to Congress, and of saying something "that absolutely can become part of a criminal or civil investigation."

"I expect Huang to take the Fifth on most questions" from Congress, Jonathan Kanter, a former assistant attorney general in the DOJ's Antitrust Division, told CNBC, meaning Huang will likely use his constitutional right against giving testimony that could be used against him in a criminal case.

"With a Justice Department criminal investigation into Megaspeed already underway and more investigations possible, anything he tells Congress could be used against him," Kanter said. "His lawyers are likely to advise him against taking that risk."

Ben Heineman Jr., a former senior vice president and general counsel of General Electric, and a senior fellow at Harvard University, told CNBC that Nvidia's board will be scrutinized over its actions before it pushed Huang to resign.

Heineman cited the board's apparent lack of succession planning and what looks like weak oversight of Huang, including relating to personal conduct such as the two alleged affairs with Nvidia employees and with an executive of an Nvidia customer.

"When there is a sort of reasonable suspicion, they need to investigate it, and they don't appear to have done that," Heineman said.

Nvidia and Huang could face investigation from state attorneys general, including in California, where the company is headquartered.

On Tuesday, California Attorney General Rob Bonta told Bloomberg Television that the situation at Nvidia with Huang is "something that we're interested in." Bonta's office later told CNBC, "We are monitoring the situation."

Huang's alleged affairs with Nvidia employees also open the possibility of workplace litigation.

Ari Wilkenfeld, a Washington-area employment lawyer, said a consensual relationship between a chief executive and a subordinate "is not in and of itself illegal."

"The liability never comes from the existence of the relationship alone. It comes from whether it was genuinely welcome, what job benefits flowed from it, and what it did to everyone else in the building," Wilkenfeld said. "California law recognizes that so-called sexual favoritism can create a hostile environment for an entire workforce.

"The affair is Huang's problem," Wilkenfeld said. "What, if anything, Nvidia did once it knew is the company's problem."
